Carrie Coon on ‘The Gilded Age’, ‘The Liberation’, and Parenting

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Carrie Coon and I are supposed to be talking about her new film, The Liberation—a gripping drama about a suburban mother who leaves her family to join an all-woman cult in the 1980s. But when we meet for tea at the London West Hollywood hotel, it doesn’t take long for our conversation to turn to general doom and gloom. Between the moment I order a custom-made ginger tea—which I’m now calling “the Carrie Coon special”—and when it arrives at our table, we’ve already started talking about fascism in America, how hard it is for an actor to find enough work to earn health insurance, global warming, and the devastating fires in Los Angeles.

“I don’t do small talk anymore. I’m not good at it,” says Coon, taking a sip from her own ginger tea—a blend she started having this restaurant make for her after getting hooked on it while filming The White Lotus in Thailand. “I’m just like, ‘So we’re all going to die. How you feeling about that?’”

But even when she’s talking fire and brimstone, Coon’s charming warmth keeps the conversation from being too dark and stormy. If Coon were leading a cult, I might join. She’s funny and empathetic; she makes this sweet, quick “aww” sound if you compliment her or share something she’s sympathetic with. Maybe Coon, who was raised in Ohio, simply can’t shed her Midwestern sensibility, even though she transforms onscreen into characters with much harder, and sometimes pricklier, outer shells. Coon has earned four Emmy nominations for playing the stubborn small-town cop Gloria Burgle in Fargo, the lonely lawyer Laurie Duffy in The White Lotus, and twice for the ambitious social climber Bertha Russell on The Gilded Age, which just debuted its fourth season on HBO.

Carrie Coon and Morgan Spector in season 2 of The Gilded Age.

She reveals a different side of herself in The Liberation, Guy Nattiv’s drama that opens in limited (and Oscar-qualifying) release November 6 before going wide January 8. Her character, Rita Cooper, is a Holocaust survivor whose trauma and stagnation in her family life cause her to abandon her husband (Tracy Letts, Coon’s real-life husband) and two daughters (Bella Ramsey and Odessa Young) for an all-female spiritual group led by a charismatic leader named Ricki (Lily James).

The film is a strong showcase for Coon as she transforms both physically and emotionally. When Rita joins the commune, she loses weight and her identity as she falls further under Ricki’s spell. “People are struck by me playing someone who feels weaker or more frail, because I’m often put into roles where I’m strong, verbal, intellectual, brassy,” says Coon. “I think it’s unusual for them to see that level of vulnerability in my performances. But of course, I think of myself as capable of all that.”

I nervously admit to Coon that when I watched The Liberation, I was troubled to find that, as a mother of two young kids, I could understand Rita’s choice—to leave behind all the demands and burdens of family and household, to run to a place where you could focus solely on yourself. It’s hard to say this out loud.

Coon makes that sympathetic “aww” sound. “We all have that moment. It’s a fantasy, but haven’t we all thought, ‘I could leave right now; what would happen if I just didn’t come back?’” she says. “That’s why I found her so compelling, because she walked away from her family and chose to invest in her self-actualization and her healing in the ’80s. It’s a really risky choice, but, I suppose, just undeniable for her.”

Coon and Letts, who married in 2013—in a hospital where Letts had just received emergency gallbladder surgery—have two kids together who are now five and eight years old, just a few years ahead of my own. Juggling life as a working mother can be challenging, especially in invisible ways. “I think a lot of the cognitive load is so heavy, and people don’t really understand,” says Coon.

Carrie Coon, Leslie Bibb, and Michelle Monaghan in The White Lotus.

When Coon was cast in the third season of HBO’s hit The White Lotus, playing a woman who goes on a luxury vacation with her two longtime best friends, she was gone for a total of 10 weeks. At the time, her daughter was only three. “That was very hard, and that was a very difficult decision,” she says. “I’m not sure it was a great decision, ultimately. I don’t know if we would do it again the same way.”

She’s had other moments of feeling spread too thin, especially just before she went to film His Three Daughters—the 2023 movie that cast Coon, Natasha Lyonne, and Elizabeth Olsen as three sisters who reunite to care for their ailing father. “I turned to Tracy and said, ‘I can’t do this anymore. I can’t do both things,’” she remembers. Maybe as her kids get older— “little kids, little problems, big kids, big problems,” she says—she will actually have to shift her focus. “In this world where we have no schema to navigate the challenges they’re facing, I would have to take a step back again and be with them,” she says. “I don’t know. It’s hard to say.”

Elizabeth Olsen and Carrie Coon in His Three Daughters.

Coon and Letts’s kids joined them for part of the time while they filmed The Liberation in Vancouver. During the first half of the movie, Coon wore hip pads and other body-shaping additions to make Rita seem more weighed down by her responsibility and the trauma of having been a child in a concentration camp. Coon says she could also lean into the exhaustion she already felt: “We were co-sleeping in a little apartment, and it was not easy,” she says. “And so I was like, ‘Great, I don’t have to look like anything. I could just be tired.’ So I kind of allowed that exhaustion I normally feel, which I try to overcome in my daily life, just to creep in more.”

When Rita leaves her family for the isolated commune, Coon’s environment also shifted: She moved to a new filming location, and her family left town. In the mornings, she’d swim or stretch. She’d also lost 10 pounds and shorn her hair for the role, transforming from Rita into Naya—the new name Ricki gives her character, a common practice in cults. “Carrie’s a very, very strong, physical actress,” says Nattiv. “Rita is the matriarch of her family. She’s a strong woman, intelligent—and then she’s not. She loses everything. It was amazing to see how she shifts between Naya and Rita.”

Nattiv based The Liberation on his grandmother Rita—who really had been in a concentration camp as a child, and joined a commune in her adult life. Rita had given testimony to Yad Vashem, the World Holocaust Remembrance Center; Coon watched it as she prepared to play the role. She also watched and listened to hours of testimonies of women who had been in concentration camps in Poland, learning “the sort of very specific way that particular trauma is held in the body, the challenge of speaking the unspeakable.”

I wondered if watching so many hours of these stories, so much horror and pain, was difficult. “Well, if I’m really honest, I found that I was drawn to listening to them instead of the news,” says Coon. “I started listening to them as a comfort, because they were a story of resilience. Thinking about the unspeakable atrocities that were happening around me in my very own country with my own tax dollars—and yet here are people on the other side of something like that, articulating those stories and what they learned.”

The present political climate is ever-present in Coon’s mind, as well as on her social media accounts. (A few of her more recent X posts: “Failed president,” “Close the camps,” “The president has nothing but ‘tranny’ jokes. How pathetic. How empty.”) For the past few weeks, she’s been encouraging people to vote, especially her fellow Ohioans. As she admitted, she’s not one for small talk.

Carrie Coon (center) with Odessa Young, Bella Ramsey, Sharon Taylor, and Tracy Letts in The Liberation.

“It’s absurd. It’s untenable,” she says. “We’re in a really precarious moment because people’s lives are untenable all across the board.” She recognizes her own privilege—that she and her husband are both able to work, that they can afford childcare—but says she’s worried for actors who can’t find roles in this shrinking economy. “Even just the labor of making tapes, the free labor, there’s no protection,” she says. “We have these rules in our union, but no one’s really following those rules. What young actor is going to make that phone call and complain?”

She’s also noticing how few female-centered films get told on the biggest stages, with the biggest budgets. “What happens when you’re living in an authoritarian regime is everybody gets scared, and everybody gets safe, and everyone’s making really safe choices. It’s not what people want,” she says. “Women are tremendous economic drivers in this economy, and we still aren’t making art for them.”

Coon will do her part, with films like The Liberation. She’s wrapped work on another independent movie, Honeymoon/Funeral, a romantic comedy from Fresh Off the Boat creator Nahnatchka Khan that also stars Rebecca Ferguson and Greta Lee. Next year, Letts heads to London from April through October to do Death of a Salesman at The National, which means more maneuvering for their family unit. “We’re thrilled for him. And yet that’s going to be a really interesting time for our family,” she says. “So I don’t have as much work lined up, and I’m okay to be patient. I feel good about being patient right now.”

But she does have one wish for the future: She’d like to do a comedy. “No one’s letting me do that. My family is surprised by my career because it’s just not the way I occurred to them as a person at all. People are expecting to meet a very serious and lugubrious actress that needs a lot of special drinks or something,” she says. While taking another sip from her custom, off-menu ginger tea.

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

The distorted, bass-heavy production that opens the latest Future single “Ya Hit” could soundtrack the beginning of a grand battle sequence in a film. Fittingly, the rapper brings out his best fighters for the record, which features appearances from Drake, Travis Scott, and Loe Shimmy.

Future announced “Ya Hit” earlier this week, but never mentioned Drake and Scott were among his collaborators. In the caption of his Instagram post, he refers to the whole team as the Avengers. Shimmy leads the chorus, while Drake punctuates his lyrics with ad-libs from Sexyy Red.

“Caught a ho lackin’, went took back the G-Wag’, ya hit/Told a lil’ ho, “Go and find you a new daddy,” ya hit/Been up for a week ’bout to drop me a new bag, ya hit/Try to play with the zombie, you know you get hit, ya hit,” Future raps on his verse.

The single marks the first release from Future since the arrival of his 10th solo studio album The Real Me in August. “Future can’t avoid being symptomatic of a mainstream rap industry that feels artistically exhausted, too uninspired to imagine new vistas beyond the old “bitches”/drugs/wealth dichotomy, and desperate for crossover gimmicks to spark waning audience interest,” Rolling Stone wrote in a review of the record. Fans joked that Future’s surprise release of a song with both Drake and Scott could threaten to overshadow album release day for Lil Baby, who shared his fifth album Fresh today. ‘Chicago P.D’ Is Officially Changing a Key Formula Next Week

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Last year, Derek Haas took his talents to Prime Video for the new crime thriller series, Countdown, which stars Jensen Ackles. The show came out of the gate with poor reviews, and while it was able to turn things around throughout the course of its first season, it was still canceled after only one season. Haas, who is also known for his work writing other projects over the years such as Christian Bale and Russell Crowe’s 3:10 to Yuma, has plenty of other things to fall back on, even though he won’t be writing another season of Countdown. The series of projects that take up most of Derek Haas’ time is One Chicago, which consists of the trio of interweaving procedurals, Chicago Fire, Chicago Med, and Chicago P.D. Each show has been on the air for over 10 years.

Chicago Fire, which premiered in 2012, has been around the longest, and Chicago Med is the newest show, having premiered in 2015. Between the two, NBC introduced Chicago P.D., which features stars like Jason Beghe as Frank Voight, who has also starred in other procedural shows such as Law & Order: SVU. Chicago P.D. is now in the heat of its 14th season, and the next episode is set to arrive on NBC on November 11 before it begins streaming on Peacock the following day. NBC even released a new description of the next episode, which teases that the officers will have to use a “different style of community policing” while working a kidnapping case in Chicago’s Chinatown area, which officially changes the formula they've used for so many years.

The ensemble cast for Chicago P.D. consists of Jason Beghe as Hank Voight, Marina Squerciati as Kim Burgess, Patrick John Flueger as Adam Ruzek, Amy Morton as Trudy Platt, LaRoyce Hawkins as Kevin Atwater, Jesse Lee Soffer as Jay Halstead, and Tracy Spiridakos as Hailey Upton. The fate of Chicago P.D. beyond Season 14 has not yet been decided, but it would be quite surprising if NBC decided to uproot the show while continuing its counterparts. What would be even more shocking is if all three were canceled while they remain three of the most-watched shows on network TV, so it’s a safe bet they’ll be around for at least a few more years.

Check out new episodes of Chicago P.D. every week on NBC and stay tuned to Collider for more updates and coverage of the One Chicago franchise.

Chicago P.D. is a gripping police procedural drama series that debuted in 2014, set against the backdrop of the city of Chicago. The show focuses on the officers and detectives of the 21st District, comprising two distinct units: the uniformed patrol officers and the Intelligence Unit that tackles major offenses.

Netflix announces Stranger Things prequel, set to debut in 2028

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

The Stranger Things universe is expanding.

On Nov. 6, Netflix announced Other Worlds: A Stranger Things Adventure, a prequel to the original hit series as well as stage show Stranger Things: The First Shadow.

Set for a 2028 release, Other Worlds comes from original series creators the Duffer Brothers, as well as Stranger Things writer and The First Shadow playwright Kate Trefry. The announcement comes as part of Stranger Things Day, as Nov. 6 marks the anniversary of Will Byers' (Noah Schnapp) disappearance in 1981.

"We’ve been dreaming of Other Worlds: A Stranger Things Adventure for many years now, and to see it become a reality is surreal and exhilarating," the Duffer Brothers said in a statement. "It's Stranger Things, but also thrillingly fresh, with a new setting, new heroes and villains, and new … actually, we're not allowed to say any more. Now, on to casting. In the meantime — watch the skies, nerds!"

Aside from the tease of a location that probably won't be Hawkins, Indiana, and new characters, plot details for Other Worlds are fairly thin. However, the teaser video accompanying the announcement offers up a few clues. The video shows an abandoned bicycle outside what appears to be a government facility, not unlike Hawkins Lab. From there, a title card appears, reading "April 19, 1955. Marion, Nevada."

The timeline and setting are intriguing, as Dr. Martin Brenner's (Matthew Modine) Nevada-based experiments to learn more about Dimension X (aka the Abyss) play a key part in Henry Creel's (Jamie Campbell Bower) backstory in both The First Shadow and Stranger Things Season 5. However, those experiments took place in Rachel, Nevada, not the fictional town of Marion.

Could Other Worlds be exploring an entirely new set of experiments tied to the Upside Down? We'll have to wait until 2028 to find out.

Other Worlds: A Stranger Things Adventure hits Netflix in 2028.

Megan Skiendel of KATSEYE Reveals the Real Reason for the Recent String of Concert Cancellations

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

The group postponed three shows after previously citing Megan’s illness.

It’s no secret that KATSEYE’s Wildworld Tour has been...a bit rocky, to say the least. With two of its members—Sophia Laforteza and Manon Bannerman—taking extended breaks from performing with the global girl group, the four remaining members (Daniela Avanzini, Lara Raj, Megan Skiendel, and Yoonchae) kicked off the tour’s North American leg on October 13, only to end up canceling their shows in Detroit, Minneapolis, and—most recently—Chicago.

The first cancellation occurred on November 3, less than an hour before the group was set to perform at Little Caesars Arena in Detroit. At the time, the group released an Instagram Story apology, saying the show was “postponed due to illness” and that “Megan is unwell and unable to perform.”

Two days later, on November 5, the official Instagram pages for Chicago’s United Center and Minneapolis’ Target Center shared statements announcing the postponements, once again citing Megan’s illness.

Not long after, the official KATSEYE Instagram account released another Story statement, this time explaining what was really going on.

“The label has made the decision to postpone our shows in Chicago and Minneapolis as Megan is recovering from a concussion,” the group wrote, adding that they would “announce the rescheduled dates as soon as possible.”

Soon after, Megan took to her own Stories to release a statement, telling fans, “I currently have a concussion and am doing everything I possibly can to recover from it so I can be on stage for you all again.”

She added that she’s been “strongly advised by medical professionals that I need to take this time to fully recover and come back safely.”

Quick reminder that this ^^^ all comes after KATSEYE was forced to pull out of Olivia Rodrigo’s Daisy Chain Fields festival back in August after Megan sprained her ankle during tour rehearsal. Per the band, Megan was evaluated by doctors and told to stay off her ankle and allow it to properly heal ahead of the group’s upcoming tour.

All this to say: Can we all agree that these women need (and deserve) an extended break??

‘I Play Rocky’ Celebrates Sylvester Stallone the Screenwriter — but Rocky Balboa Was Only the Beginning

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

In “I Play Rocky,” screenwriter Peter Gamble and director Peter Farrelly tell the unlikely true story of how an unemployed actor named Sylvester Stallone wrote “Rocky” and turned himself into one of the biggest movie stars in the world. Like “Rocky” itself, it’s an inspiring and extremely entertaining crowd-pleaser; it also devotes a surprising amount of screen time to Stallone’s writing process and the machinations required to actually get his screenplay produced — the movie doesn’t get to the actual production of “Rocky” until over an hour into its running time.

Even though it’s a love letter to Stallone’s script, “I Play Rocky” presents his screenwriting aspirations less as an all-consuming goal than as a means to an end, a way for the young actor to create parts for himself when no one else would. Yet not only did Stallone keep writing after “Rocky” made him a movie star, he continued to do so at a pace that would shame all but the most prolific screenwriters. Stallone has writing credits on over 20 produced features — and not all of them were vehicles for himself as an actor. Related Stories Inside ‘Godzilla Minus Zero’: How an R Rating, Filming in IMAX, and Epic Kaiju Battles Broke Franchise Tradition Rosamund Pike Starring in Body Horror Film ‘Feed’ for ‘Star Wars: The Acolyte’ Creator Leslye Headland

As early as 1983 (when he wrote the “Saturday Night Fever” sequel “Staying Alive” sequel at John Travolta’s behest) and as recently as last year (when he wrote “A Working Man” for his “Expendables” costar Jason Statham), Stallone has sat behind his keyboard to script films for other performers, indicating that there was always a deep drive to write that went beyond the need to self-generate acting work. Factor in that he wrote or co-wrote every “Rocky” and “Rambo” movie and even worked on the scripts for one-off oddities like “Rhinestone,” and it becomes clear that Stallone’s been nearly as single-minded about writing as “I Play Rocky” depicts him when it comes to acting.

After “Rocky” won the Oscar for Best Picture (Stallone was nominated for his screenplay but had the bad luck to be up against Paddy Chayefsky for “Network”), Stallone no longer had to write to find parts — he was presumably getting acting offers thrown at him every time he picked up the phone. Yet he followed “Rocky” with two movies that established the precedent for the career to come, the labor drama “F.I.S.T.” and the wrestling picture “Paradise Alley,” on both of which the actor did double duty as a writer.

“Paradise Alley” was a passion project, a script Stallone had written before “Rocky” that he now had the juice to get made — and on which he made his directorial debut. “F.I.S.T.” was something different, a movie that came Stallone’s way in the form of a screenplay by Rolling Stone journalist Joe Eszterhas. The authorship of the script became a bone of contention when Stallone worked with director Norman Jewison to pare Eszterhas’ work down for production; Stallone thought he deserved a screenwriting credit, Eszterhas didn’t. According to Eszterhas, Stallone strong-armed him into sharing credit by threatening to withhold approval for a novelization that would net Eszterhas a boatload of cash; after sparring in the press the two writers reconciled, became friends, and collaborated years later on the notorious flop “Burn Hollywood Burn: An Allan Smithee Film.”

For the rest of the 1970s and 1980s, then more sporadically in the decades to follow, Stallone would follow the “F.I.S.T.”/”Paradise Alley” pattern, alternating rewrites of movies he came on to as an actor like “First Blood” and “Cliffhanger” with passion projects he originated like the “Rocky” and “Rambo” sequels and “Driven.” “Rocky II” followed “Paradise Alley” in 1979, and it was essentially a remake as much as a sequel, with Rocky Balboa once again finding himself the underdog up against Apollo Creed. It was also hugely successful at the box office, though slightly less so with critics who saw it as an embalming of the original.

If Stallone was bothered by reviewers like Janet Maslin calling “Rocky II” lifeless, he made his retort in the form of the propulsive “Rocky III,” which refined the “Rocky” formula and created the screenwriting sensibility that would define the majority of Stallone’s 1980s output. “Rocky III” strips its plot down to the bone — Rocky loses a fight, gets humiliated, trains rigorously, then comes roaring back in triumph — but then inflates it again with muscular melodrama. The motives and rooting interest are uncomplicated and impossible to misinterpret, and each straightforward emotional beat is delivered with such sincere and amplified force that resistance is futile.

This approach would reach peak effectiveness in “Rocky IV,” a film that both simplifies the formula to its barest essence (there’s barely any actual plot in it at all) and builds it up to have global consequences, as Rocky’s match against Russian boxer Ivan Drago is presented as nothing less than a battle to determine the winner of the Cold War. Before Stallone could write “Rocky IV,” however, he got an intriguing offer from fellow movie star John Travolta — would he be interested in writing and directing the sequel to Travolta’s 1977 smash “Saturday Night Fever?”

Travolta’s years-long attempt to bring back Brooklyn disco dancer Tony Manero had gone nowhere; none of the writers he worked with saw the character or the movie the way he did, and he was growing frustrated. When Travolta saw “Rocky III,” he realized Stallone was a filmmaker with the narrative and visual energy he needed for his sequel. Stallone was game, and together he and Travolta crafted “Staying Alive,” a crowd-pleaser in which Tony Manero would trade the disco floor for the Broadway stage.

Much as Stallone had minimized the grittiness of the original “Rocky” in the slicker “Rocky III,” “Staying Alive” ditched the racist and misogynistic characters of “Saturday Night Fever” (and their relatively bleak prospects) in favor of a rousing showbiz tale in the tradition of “42nd Street” and “Footlight Parade.” Critics scoffed, but audiences loved it — “Staying Alive” was one of the biggest hits of 1983, and after a brief stumble with “Rhinestone,” an ill-advised musical from the director of “Porky’s” pairing Stallone and Dolly Parton, Stallone the screenwriter came back with three smash hits in a row.

1985 represented a peak year for Stallone as both writer and star, with the release of two of his most successful pictures, “Rambo: First Blood Part II” (cowritten with a young James Cameron) and “Rocky IV.” Then came 1986’s “Cobra,” the cop movie in which Stallone’s screenwriting voice reached its fullest apotheosis and purest form. The film grew out of Stallone’s aborted stint playing Axel Foley in “Beverly Hills Cop”; Stallone drastically rewrote the screenplay before leaving the project, but when Eddie Murphy came on board and the tone shifted from action to comedy, Stallone’s material was scrapped.

Stallone reworked his discarded scenes into “Cobra,” a blatant “Dirty Harry” ripoff that replicates the vigilante formula of that film and its sequels but removes every trace of their ambiguity. In “Dirty Harry,” “Magnum Force,” and “Sudden Impact,” parallels are drawn between Eastwood’s detective Harry Callahan and the villains to suggest that the cop and his prey are not as far as apart as Callahan would like to believe. “Cobra” doesn’t entertain such moral complexity for a single second — from the moment the first bad guy is introduced parking in a handicapped spot to later revelations about other villains sexually assaulting children, it’s made clear that Stallone’s Lieutenant Marion “Cobra” Cobretti is completely justified doing whatever he has to to wipe these guys off of the planet.

Just as Stallone pared down the structures of his own “Rocky” and “Rambo” films to the essentials in their sequels, with “Cobra” he takes the iconography of the “Dirty Harry” movies — right from the “Magnum Force”-inspired pre-credits sequence in which Stallone provides crime statistics in voice-over as a gun fires into the camera — and expresses it with pure id, stoking the audience’s bloodlust to a fever pitch. This is not a criticism. “Cobra” is, in its way, as perfect a script as “Chinatown,” a supremely engineered entertainment machine without a wasted line or shot. While some of this was reportedly dictated by commercial concerns — the movie was cut down to its lean 87-minute running time in an effort to squeeze in an extra showtime each day — the ruthless efficiency is so characteristic of virtually every movie Stallone ever wrote that it can’t really be arbitrary.

Stallone the screenwriter would follow “Cobra” with “Rambo III” and “Rocky V,” two of the weaker entries in their respective franchises, but 1993’s “Cliffhanger” was a truly perfect action movie that revived Stallone’s career as an actor after a pair of comedy flops, John Landis’ criminally underrated “Oscar” and the merely criminal “Stop! Or My Mom Will Shoot.” After that Stallone slowed down as a screenwriter and focused more on acting — he didn’t take another writing credit after “Cliffhanger” until “Driven,” another collaboration with “Cliffhanger” director Renny Harlin, in 2001.

Stallone returned to his typewriter (or, presumably by this time, laptop) in 2006 to revisit Rocky Balboa for the sequel of the same name, and the project seems to have reignited something in the writer’s artistic soul. “Rocky Balboa” begins a new phase for Stallone as a writer, one in which he switches from trying to perfect his formulas to pondering their purposes and effects. This film and the “Rambo” sequels that would follow are among Stallone’s best work as a screenwriter, and they tend to be overlooked given that they fall outside of his most popular era as a movie star.

“Rocky Balboa” doesn’t abandon the franchise’s standard tropes, but it pokes around at their edges to rediscover the humanity that made the original “Rocky” such a hit. Rousing and deeply heartfelt, it’s a formula movie that rarely feels clichéd thanks to Stallone’s genuine love for his characters and his determination to end the series on a high note. At the time, after a series of underperforming and undernourished films, Stallone had as much to prove as his onscreen alter ego, and in “Rocky Balboa” he wipes his slate of artistic and commercial failures clean with 102 minutes of unadulterated cinematic passion.

As “Rocky Balboa” begins, the title character is living a comfortable but dull life as a restauranteur, and mourning the death of his beloved wife Adrian. Desperate to prove to himself that he still has what it takes, Rocky applies for a new boxing license and, through a series of slightly unconvincing but quickly paced circumstances, ends up in the ring with the current heavyweight champion of the world. The boxing story is a lot of fun, and as a director Stallone stages the final match with energy and style, but the movie’s real heart is in the scenes leading up to the fight.

As usual, Stallone packs a surprising number of subplots and emotional high points into the film’s compact running time, and he’s able to do this by building on what has happened in the previous films via a kind of cinematic shorthand. He doesn’t need to waste a lot of time on what Rocky has lost or where he has come from, because those of us who have seen the other movies can fill in the blanks. Stallone uses his alter ego to explore all the things that matter to him as an aging legend: missed opportunities, lost and found loved ones, and the desire to give what he was born to do one last big shot.

It’s impossible to avoid the parallels between Balboa’s story and Stallone’s own, but this isn’t a case of an actor playing himself — he and Rocky may have the same feelings, but they express them in completely different ways. One of the pleasures of the first two Rocky films was Stallone’s blunt, authentic dialogue writing, and his talent for clumsy but entirely appropriate urban speech returns in “Rocky Balboa.” The film contains several significant relationships, from the expected sparring with brother-in-law Paulie to a new romance and a sweet father-son love story, and in each of these storylines the dialogue is pitch-perfect.

Early on Stallone figured out how to write in such a way that Rocky communicates his emotions with total clarity even though he’s an uneducated, inarticulate character, and the style reaches its zenith in “Rocky Balboa.” This kind of screenwriting is awfully tricky, but Stallone never gets the balance wrong; less astute viewers might mistakenly assume that the awkward dialogue is stupid or unintentionally obvious, but it’s exactly right for this character and this story.

After “Rocky Balboa” got Stallone’s creative juices flowing again, his output as a screenwriter resumed; in 2010 he created a whole new franchise with “The Expendables,” and scripted two solid Jason Statham vehicles, “Homefront” and “A Working Man.” He even returned to Rocky Balboa one more time for “Creed II.” Stallone’s best late-career screenwriting efforts, however, were devoted to his other iconic character, Vietnam vet and one-man killing machine John Rambo. In fact, the last two entries in that series are so good they recalibrate our entire understanding of the character and the franchise as a whole.

That the “Rambo” series became one of the more durable franchises in American cinema is a bit surprising in retrospect, given that it didn’t really find its voice until the fourth installment and began with a film that didn’t lend itself to sequels at all. By the time Stallone returned to the saga in 2008 for the simply titled “Rambo,” he had clearly spent years thinking about the character and where his own ambitions for the franchise might intersect with his audience’s expectations.

The result was a movie in which Stallone was able to have it both ways, digging deeper into the self-doubt and unremitting pessimism that always made John Rambo an unusually interesting mass appeal action hero while also delivering bloody set pieces that made “Dawn of the Dead” look like “Driving Miss Daisy.” The most recent entry in the cycle, 2019’s “Rambo: Last Blood,” continues along those lines with a tale that brings Stallone’s ongoing philosophical inquiry into the possibility of meaningful action in a meaningless world to its fullest fruition.

“Last Blood” was underestimated during its initial theatrical release, including by me — I didn’t fully grasp its greatness until revisiting the entire series. Watching the films in order I was surprised to find that for all the stylistic disparities between the movies, they do have a common thread in Stallone’s depiction of Rambo as a hero who doesn’t really like himself or what he has become — the cumulative emotional impact is comparable to what might have happened had Clint Eastwood decided to make a whole series of movies about his William Munny character from “Unforgiven.”

The authentic turmoil that Stallone conveys gives even the cartoonish second and third “Rambo” films more depth than they’re normally given credit for, and undercuts their reputations as simplistic expressions of rah-rah Reagan-era patriotism. (In championing the films, Reagan showed that he misunderstood them almost as badly as he misunderstood Bruce Springsteen’s “Born in the U.S.A.”) Stallone’s status as an eighties action icon alongside Arnold Schwarzenegger and Chuck Norris has overpowered his actual appeal and value as both an actor and a writer in the films that made him a star; his specialty isn’t invulnerable supermen but haunted antiheroes plagued by regret.

James Mangold understood this about Stallone and used the actor’s traumatized quality to enormous advantage in “Copland,” a movie for which Stallone the actor deservedly received his best reviews since the original “Rocky.” Mangold was one of the few filmmakers who really knew how to use Stallone to his maximum potential, though others (like Richard Donner in the underrated “Assassins”) occasionally came close, and Stallone recently pulled off a late triumph with Taylor Sheridan’s “Tulsa King.” For the most part, though, Stallone the actor has always been best served by Stallone the writer — not just in the days documented by “I Play Rocky,” but throughout his career.

Jimmy Stewart Biopic Officially Honors Service Members and Veterans With New Release

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

KJ Apa may have missed out on Marvel’s new X-Men project, but he’s officially back in theaters with the long-awaited Jimmy Stewart biopic this weekend. During a press session for the film, Apa revealed exclusively to Collider that he screen-tested for a role in Marvel’s X-Men reboot, which is currently scheduled to be released in theaters on May 5, 2028. While he didn't reveal which role he auditioned for, there is rampant fan speculation that it was either Gambit or Cyclops — the rebooted version of Gambit has not yet been announced, but Kit Connor has been cast as the new Cyclops. Apa was once only known for his role as Archie in Riverdale, but he’s since blossomed into one of Hollywood’s brightest up-and-coming stars with plenty of other exciting roles in the works.

However, for KJ Apa, none of that can start until he finishes the press tour for Jimmy, which is finally out now in theaters after months of promotion and anticipation. The movie is set at the peak of Jimmy Stewart’s stardom, after he had just won the Academy Award for his role in The Philadelphia Story in 1941. He then chose to leave his Hollywood life behind and enlist in the Army Air Corps, where he flew over 20 combat missions before rising to the rank of Colonel. It also explores how his service helped give It's a Wonderful Life its impact that still resonates 80 years later.

This afternoon, the team behind Jimmy officially announced a global base buyout for service members and their families, courtesy of the Timothy T. Day Foundation and Lockheed Martin. Free screenings to see Jimmy will take place on opening night for Soldiers, Sailors, Airmen, Marines, Guardians, and Coast Guardsmen and their families at the same time that viewers around the world are going to theaters to see the movie. An exclusive clip of the U.S. Air Forces in Europe Band in Jimmy can be found below.

Jimmy Stewart’s daughter, Kelly Stewart Harcourt, released an official statement about the film, not only providing her full support for the story it tells, but also backing the collaboration between the film’s creative team and the Stewart family:

“I truly believe that my father would not only have approved of the film, JIMMY, but he would have been proud of the tribute it pays to his wartime service. He would have been especially moved by the collaboration between the film’s producers and the many organizations who have enabled our military, veterans, and first responders and their families to see JIMMY for free,” said Jimmy’s daughter and the film’s executive producer, Kelly Stewart Harcourt. “If Dad were alive today, he would say, with his hand to his heart, ‘This means more to me than I can tell you.’”

Jimmy was written by Justin Strawhand and directed by Aaron Burns, and the film holds a solid 65% from critics on Rotten Tomatoes, which is less than 10% away from earning the Certified Fresh label. Additional cast members for the film include Neal McDonough as Alexander, Max Casella as Frank Capra, Kara Killmer as Lady Julia, Jason Alexander as Louis B. Mayer, and Rob Riggle as Colonel Terrill. Neal McDonough also serves as one of the executive producers of Jimmy.

Check out Jimmy in theaters around the world and stay tuned to Collider for more updates and coverage of the film.

Keanu Reeves Officially Returning as John Wick in New Movie

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Though John Wick seemingly met his end in John Wick: Chapter 4, Keanu Reeves isn't done with his stylish assassin character just yet. A fifth John Wick live-action movie is in development, and director Chad Stahelski is also working on an animated prequel film. Now, Reeves is officially confirmed to return for it.

Lionsgate greenlit the film last April, and Reeves recently closed his deal to return. The movie will recount John Wick's "Impossible Task" — the night he killed all his rivals as a way of freeing himself from the High Table. It will be directed by Shannon Tindle (Ultraman: Rising) and co-written by Ryan and Kaz Firpo (Eternals). The animation studio has not been announced.

John Wick was introduced in 2014 with a self-contained action flick. Its success launched a franchise, and the lore of the series expanded in each subsequent outing. Reeves has starred in all four John Wick films and appeared alongside Ana de Armas in the 2025 spinoff Ballerina.

Donnie Yen is starring in another spinoff focused on Caine, the blind assassin he played in John Wick: Chapter 4. That film recently wrapped principal photography. The series made the move to streaming TV in 2023 with The Continental: From the World of John Wick, which focused on the hotel that caters to assassins.

No release date has been announced yet for the John Wick animated film.

5 HBO Mystery Shows With No Bad Seasons, Ranked

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

HBO is home to a number of the greatest TV dramas of all time, from The Sopranos, to The Wire, to Succession. HBO's drama series stand out for their unique premises that draw viewers in immediately, as well as strong and compellingly flawed characters that have captivating arcs over the course of their shows. From workplace dramas, to crime dramas, to fantasy and sci-fi series, HBO's drama series are all different from each other, yet they're each gripping and emotionally resonant.

Amongst its phenomenal drama shows, HBO boasts a number of excellent mystery series. While many of these are miniseries, a select handful of HBO's mystery series have multiple seasons that are each truly fantastic. These shows set up compelling mysteries early on and slowly expand on them throughout the course of each season, ultimately delivering a phenomenal payoff in every season finale. These shows also have a solid balance between their central mysteries and their focus on their characters and their core dynamics. These are HBO mystery shows with no bad seasons, ranked.

Each season of HBO's hit crime anthology series, True Detective, is vastly different. All of its seasons see its main detectives grappling with their own painful pasts while trying to catch a mysterious killer. The murder or series of murders at the center of the season is always particularly horrifying, and has a unique twist to it. This framework stays the same with each season, but they all have their own separate cast of characters, and they're each set in a different part of the United States.

True Detective now has four seasons under its belt, and its fifth is set to be released next year. True Detective had a very strong start with its gritty and captivating Southern Gothic-style first season, but each of the seasons that has followed continues to tell a compelling and shocking main story. Every season of True Detective tackles its own separate themes related to its specific setting, keeping the series feeling fresh while still keeping consistent with its core structure.

With three seasons under its belt and another already confirmed to be on the way, The White Lotus is one of HBO's best currently ongoing series. Each season of the anthology-style drama series follows a group of guests during their stay at a White Lotus hotel, a fictionalized luxury hotel chain. At the start of each season, a flash-forward reveals that at least one person was killed during this stay. The show then jumps back in time to the very start of the visit, following an ensemble cast of guests and staff. Each season takes place at a White Lotus in a different location, following a completely new group of people, with occasional minimal overlap between seasons.

The White Lotus is primarily a slowburn dark comedy, setting up compelling dynamics full of tension that builds up throughout the course of the season. All the conflict is character-driven, and these tense and occasionally hostile interactions build a sense of dread in viewers before ultimately boiling over at the end of the season. The show uses its murder mystery framing to set up the stakes at the start of each season, so that viewers know that at least one of these characters will ultimately lose their lives as a result of these conflicts that would otherwise seem low-stakes at the beginning. Every season of The White Lotus honors this framing and embraces the show's unique tone while also standing out on their own due to a new setting and cast of characters, and they're all fantastic.

Based on Charlaine Harris' The Southern Vampire Mysteries series, True Blood is a dark fantasy series set in a version of the modern real world where the existence of vampires is widely known. Due to the invention of a synthetic blood called Tru Blood, vampires can finally come out of hiding and exist as a part of society — in theory. In reality, vampires are still being ostracized by most humans, who want nothing to do with them. At worst, there are humans who actively want vampires dead, as well as some who are interested in acquiring their blood, which is a highly addictive drug to humans. Some vampires just want to blend in and be treated like anyone else, while others still want to kill and drink human blood like usual.

The series is set in the small town of Bon Temps, Louisiana, and it follows a human waitress named Sookie Stackhouse (Anna Paquin), who can read the minds of everyone around her. Because of this, Sookie has never really fit in, and she empathizes with the vampires. The series kicks off when Sookie meets and quickly falls in love with a 173-year-old vampire named Bill Compton (Stephen Moyer), despite the judgment that they both receive from nearly everyone around them. Each season of True Blood also has a compelling central mystery around which the season is framed. In the first season, someone is murdering local women who have been associated with vampires, which puts Sookie at risk as well. True Blood is a captivating and riveting series all the way through the course of its seven seasons, and its mysteries keep viewers guessing until the very end of each season.

Four houses, four completely different theories about how you survive a world like this one. Here is where your banner belongs, what the name would cost you, and what it would buy.

You belong in the North, where the weather does most of the arguing and nobody is impressed by a title. The Starks are the only great house on this list who genuinely believe that being right matters more than winning, which is exactly why the show spent eight years punishing them for it.

You belong to the house that worked out early on that gold moves faster than armies. Casterly Rock runs on leverage, reputation and the certainty that everybody at the table owes you something, and you are perfectly comfortable with all three.

You belong to the house that conquered a continent and never quite got over it. Targaryens do not ask for a seat at the table, because in their version of events the table was theirs to begin with, and that certainty is either magnificent or catastrophic depending on the week.

You belong to Highgarden, where nobody raises their voice and everybody gets exactly what they came for. The Tyrells feed the realm, charm the realm and quietly steer the realm, and they have never once needed to win a battle to get their way.

Based on the novel of the same name by Liane Moriarty, Big Little Lies follows a group of women living in Monterey, California who are all mothers to first-graders at the same elementary school: Madeline Mackenzie (Reese Witherspoon), Celeste Wright (Nicole Kidman), Jane Chapman (Shailene Woodley), Bonnie Carlson (Zoë Kravitz), and Renata Klein (Laura Dern). A flash-forward at the start of the series reveals that someone was murdered at a fundraising event for the elementary school. The series then goes back to earlier in the school year, starting on Orientation Day, where Madeline and Celeste become friends with Jane, who's new in town.

At the same time, a violent incident occurs in the first-grade class on Orientation Day, and it's unclear which child perpetrated the act. As tensions rise amongst the mothers, they all deal with the issues occurring in their own lives, both public and private. All the while, the events of the present day are framed by a narration from the night of the fundraiser, as other parents attending that night give their own perspectives on these five women and their families. The second season is a big switch-up, moving away from the central murder mystery to deal with the falling out of that night. Both seasons of Big Little Lies are absolutely fantastic, and it will be exciting to see what direction the drama series takes when it returns for Season 3.

Based on the novel of the same name by Tom Perrotta, The Leftovers is a deeply captivating mystery box show set three years after a mysterious event where 2% of the world's population suddenly disappeared without a trace. In the wake of this tragedy, the entire world struggles to move forward from this massive loss despite not having any answers as to what happened. The series centers on Kevin Garvey (Justin Theroux), who, at the start of the show, is the police chief of the small town of Mapleton, New York.

Every season of The Leftovers is wildly different but equally fantastic. They all follow a group of core characters, while also adding in new characters and leaving others behind with each new season. Season 2 jumps forward in time to the days ahead of the fourth anniversary of the Departure, and it is set in Jarden, Texas, the only place that wasn't affected by it. Season 3 is set primarily in Australia, just before the seventh anniversary of the Departure. Over the course of its three seasons, The Leftovers sets up a number of mysteries that all connect back to the Departure, brilliantly choosing when to answer these questions and when not to.

We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.

Joanna Gaines Shares Secret to Chip Gaines Marriage After 23 Years

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Joanna Gaines detailed how she and husband Chip Gaines “create magic” in their 23-year marriage, sharing what makes their relationship even "stronger" over time.

If your relationship is in need of a fixer upper, Joanna Gaines has the blueprint for you.

The HGTV star shared insight into her long-lasting marriage with Chip Gaines, revealing what she believes is the secret to their strong bond after over 25 years together.

“I love just how opposite we are,” Joanna shared on the Nov. 4 episode of the Not Gonna Lie podcast. “I do feel like once you understand that Chip’s strengths and my strengths are very different, but together, it really does create magic. There’s a tension there, but then there’s power there that really happens when you embrace it.”

“For both of us, we’re OK evolving,” she continued to host Kylie Kelce. “It’s not like, ‘This is who I am, and that’s who you are.’”

The Fixer Upper star—who shares kids Drake, 21, Ella, 19, Duke, 18, Emmie, 16, and Crew, 8, with Chip—emphasized that her husband of 23 years is “challenging me every day.”

As Joanna put it, the couple holds “each other’s differences with this respect and almost this awe.”

And the pair—who tied the knot in May 2003—lean into the less comfortable moments, even if they aren’t easy.

“Even though I call it chaos,” Joanna explained, “but finding that discomfort in every season and working through that together really does make you stronger.”

“There’s always a challenge so that we’re never just comfortable with each other,” she added. “And we’re always learning something new together.”

As for what following this mindset can look like? The Magnolia founder shared that she’s seen a “shift happening,” in which she’s becoming “more extroverted” like her husband and vice versa.

“It’s like dating all over again,” she noted, “getting to know each other.”

Ultimately, Joanna and Chip are always encouraging each other to be their best selves.

“So enjoying the unfolding, enjoying the evolution,” she shared, “but also always pushing each other to a way that’s good.”

“He really is my best friend,” the 48-year-old added. “I love it.”

To see what more stars had to say about their long-lasting marriages, keep reading…

"I never thought that I would laugh this much in my marriage. That is such a fundamental ingredient of who you are, this insistence on joy," Hermann detailed to his bride of their partnership of over 20 years in Marlo Thomas and Phil Donahue's 2020 book What Makes a Marriage Last. "And I think what sustains our marriage is that I know you love me in spite of who I am, and that is the definition of grace."Seeking out that happiness is key. Even after a particularly robust argument, "One of us will test the waters with a joke—about the very thing we were fighting about," Herman shared of life with the Law & Order: SVU star. "It's like one of us says, 'I'm not saying I was wrong, and I'm not still insisting I was entirely right, but can we at least inch our way back toward the place where we caught at stuff together?' Once that happens, it's a pretty good sign that things are on their way to getting patched up."

They've avoided need for any major renovation by sticking to the same advice they got in premarital counseling ahead of their 2003 vows. Even five kids in, Tuesday date nights are a must and they've held off on purchasing a TV, instead finding other ways to connect. But if Chip were to offer any tip to follow, it'd be to pursue the person you love "like a hornet." Some two decades in, he said, he still feels like the guy hoping to get a second date. "I'm not saying she'd never cheat on me," he explained, "but it's not going to be because I never told her I loved her or because I didn't send her flowers or I forgot our anniversary."

"My first piece of advice is not to take advice from celebrities," Bacon joked of his 36-year union. It's as succinct as their other go-to, "Keep the fights clean and the sex dirty," a phrase developed specifically to end any further chatter about their marriage. Truthfully, though, they make it a point not to let arguments linger, rarely digging in for the sake of the victory. "Honestly, we don't like to fight, so when we actually are in an argument, we're both looking for a solution," The Closer actress explained to Thomas and Donahue. "For the most part we're struggling to get back to everything being okay, because it sucks to fight." Because, when it comes down to it, she continued, "There is no Plan B. No matter what, we want to work it out."

Over 37 years into marriage, the actors have mastered the art of fighting fair. "Tracy and I don't pick scabs," explained the Family Ties alum. "In some marriages, people look at their partner and see vulnerability and they just can't help but go after that vulnerability, like it's a sport or something. We don't do that." That's not to say they don't have arguments. "If I've said something stupid, I have the tendency to want to take it back and make it all okay," he said. "But that doesn't really work." Instead, he follows her lead and tries to give her space. She, in turn, offers up understanding: "Sometimes you just have to say to yourself, 'You know what? He said something schmucky and it made me feel bad. But he's a good person and I'm going to give him the benefit of the doubt that he didn't realize that what he said hurt my feelings.'"

"He still makes me laugh more than any human being," Curtis said about the Waiting for Guffman director on Today in December 2024, ahead of the couple's 40th wedding anniversary, adding jokingly, "and I'm sure there's something about me that he likes. I don't know what it is, but I'm sure there's something."

"I think one of the things that has kept us together all of these years is that we both define relationships as something that's relatively indefinable," the How I Met Your Mother alum said. Through 21 years of career shifts (actor-chef Burtka released his cookbook Life Is a Party in 2019), parenting twins Gideon and Harper and dealing with tough times, "Marriage never stays the same," explained Harris. "When you have sex with the same person over and over, it gets redundant, and so you try different things. Then one day you don't like each other, and suddenly you're not attracted to each other, so you have to figure out how to be reattracted to them—but in a different way because you're aging."Eventually, he continued, you find yourself more attracted to their soul. And then their body again. "It all keeps morphing," he noted. "So in a weird way, we keep falling in love with each other in different ways, over and over."

Appropriately, the comic actors believe their funniness gives them life. And not just in their 19-year union itself. "Whenever we have a good laugh," noted the Can You Ever Forgive Me? actress, "especially a crazy one, when you're like, Oh, my God, and you're almost dizzy—we always assign it a specific amount of time that it added to our lives. And I'm always adding it up. I'll say, 'Okay, that was like two months—I just got two more months to live!'"They put a time limit on disagreements as well. Citing the oft-repeated don't go to bed angry rule, Falcone, said, "I tried it once, and I realized that in the morning I had forgotten what I was mad about. You're not getting any answers if you're parsing out an argument when everybody is tired and possibly had a drink or two. I've never had the thing where you're having an argument at ten o'clock at night, and then you say, 'Well, that was good. I'm glad we got to the bottom of that. We agree. Truce signed.'"

Each having wed before they found their way to the other in 1995, they not only had to navigate a marriage, but life as step-parents to two children apiece. "There is no book that tells you how to do it, so the one thing I figured out right away is that they already have a mom—and it's not me. So what did they need from me?" the Zoey's Extraordinary Playlist actor recalled. "That's when I realized that everybody needs a cheerleader, right? There's never too many of those in your life, so that's what I'll be. I never set their boundaries, disciplined them, or tried to teach them right from wrong. They have parents who do that."The Cheers alum agreed with her stance wholeheartedly. "I think that's really wise, to offer yourself as a friend," he said. "‘I'm not going to discipline you and I'm not going to judge you. What I'm going to do is hang out with you and be there for you.' And that's what you have to do: absolutely, genuinely be there."

The moment same-sex civil unions were legalized in Britain, the music icon and the Canadian ad exec were joined together in a Dec. 21, 2005 ceremony. They repeated the process on the exact same day nine years later once they were able to legally wed. And yet the anniversary they recognize is their unexpected meeting during a 1993 weekend dinner party at the singer's Windsor, England flat, his friend having set the guest list.Every Saturday, no matter where they are in the world, together or apart, the two pen a handwritten note to each other, by the authors' count, some 1,352 letters in all. "There's something very spiritual and real about handwriting," explains Furnish, "and the cards are a chance to reflect on the week that's passed and talk about the week that's coming up." Agreed the five-time Grammy winner, "It's part of the success, I think, of a lasting relationship. Communication is the most important thing."

Having literally Secreted their 40-year marriage into existence ("Six months before I met him, I had these recurring dreams about this person I was going to marry,") they make sure their union has remained front-and-center even as their entire existence has shifted. "Marriage is a priority for both of us. And that means that we act on that and refocus when we've lost sight of the ball," she said. If the surgeon could prescribe one piece of advice, it would be to place that bond above all else. "The bottom line is this: I would do anything for her. Climb any mountain, take any bullet—in the chest, too, by the way. I might do things that justifiably make her really angry at me, but I would never let anything block me from delivering my love to her," he swore. If you appreciate how valuable marriage is to your long-term happiness, he continued, "You will never let anyone touch it."

ABC News reporter Roberts isn't always one for chit-chat. "I don't like the check-in," she explaind. "If you're calling just to say, 'So, what's up?' no, I do not like that." Everyone's favorite TV weatherman, however, is a phone guy. And after years of chafing against his frequent calls, a pal proposed something that changed her stance. "One friend said to me, 'Did you ever think that maybe he just feels comfortable when he hears your voice, because that tells him that all is right in the world?'" she recounted. "And I thought, 'That's very sweet. I'd never thought about it in that way. And if it means something to him, then it should mean something to me.'"Now, she said, "I have learned to take a breath and say, 'Sweetie, I've got some stuff going on, but what's going on with you? Great. I'm glad to hear from you. Got to go. Talk to you later. Love you.' That makes all the difference in the world to him, and it doesn't kill me for two minutes to be nice and sweet." They celebrated their 29th anniversary in September 2024.

The building blocks for their nearly 30-year union began back in their newlywed days, when any fight—one involved the Riverdale actor throwing the talk show host's ring out the window—felt like it could be it. "Early in a marriage, it's easy to let little things become big things—whether it's financial strain or career strain or you have kids and you're sleep-deprived," espoused the LIVE With Kelly and Ryan star. "But Mark taught me to walk away and take a breath. That's when you figure out that it's not a marriage-defining moment."Some hard-earned wisdom, to be sure, but now the parents of three are reaping the benefits. "Anytime you see a couple who seems truly happy, you can bet they've gone through some crazy, crazy stuff together and they've survived," he stated. "That's something to be proud of."

She's an introvert, she said, "maybe a step away from being a straight-up loner"; he's the ultimate extrovert "the mayor of everywhere," as he put it. She's a touch messy; he's "a little OCD" noted the Oscar winner. But, wed since 2003, they've long since learned to let the other do their thing. That's the advice the How to Get Away With Murder lead said she gives to all her soon-to-be-wed friends. "Marriage does not start when you walk down the aisle," she shared. "Your marriage starts when you look over at a person who you love more than anything, and there's something about him—just one character trait that makes you say to yourself, 'Oh man, that's going to drive me crazy. I don't know if I can deal with this.' And then the next minute you say, 'But you know what? I love him.' That's when your marriage starts."

You don't reach the 50-year mark in any relationship by letting disagreements drag on. Any time there's a blowup, noted the Grace and Frankie star, "Usually, I'm the one who apologizes. It's not hard because I love her and can't bear for her to feel lonely for even five minutes." Even better is when she can avoid saying she's sorry to the writer altogether. Her top takeaway, she shared, "Remember, when you're angry at your partner and say something hurtful, you will be more angry at yourself later for having said hurtful things to the person you love. You'll feel angry twice. Not good for your blood pressure, and certainly not good for your relationship."

Few things are more on brand than former New York State Supreme Court judge Jerry declaring their decades-long union works because he usually lets the Judge Judy icon win. But for his bride it's more about knowing you're not always going to like the final verdict. Their 12-year marriage dissolved in 1990 when he couldn't be the caretaker she needed following her father's death. Yet, when they got back together one year later and quickly remarried, she had no delusions that he was suddenly going to be the type to run the household or take the lead on birthday plans. "Every relationship is different, but there is a common thread of unhappiness, and that unhappiness comes from trying to make another person different from who they are. You can try, but they're always going to resent it," she explained matter-of-factly. "I don't think you should marry anyone with the expectation of changing who they are."

The Lost alum would be, well, lost without his longtime love, who he married in 1993. As he explained to E! News in April 2025, "My wife—being patient as I traipse around the world, going from job to job—she's kept our family stable. She's been fantastic."

Calling her a "very patient woman," the actor said his wife and their two sons keeps him "humble no matter what's happening."

"They shape my values," he added. "It's great to have that perspective and North Star."

The Biggest High Fantasy Series Of The Decade Officially Returns In 1 Week After Key Change

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

A major high fantasy series is returning next week, and it just so happens to be the genre's biggest release of the decade, which is coming back with a new set of episodes that are unlike its previous seasons due to a couple of important factors. There have been many successful fantasy TV shows in recent years, with Game of Thrones' popularity boosting the genre.

After all, the HBO fantasy series grew to become one of the most popular TV shows of all time, with multiple Game of Thrones spin-offs having since been released. Elsewhere, the fantasy genre has remained strong with shows like Netflix's The Witcher in live-action and Arcane in animation, Prime Video's The Wheel of Time in live-action and both The Legend of Vox Machina and The Mighty Nein in animation, and more.

Those last two animated series might be the perfect examples in recent years of how to do right by high fantasy in TV format. The Prime Video fantasy shows are set in Critical Role's world of Exandria, coming with immersive worldbuilding, deep and intricate lore, fantastical creatures, magic systems, and more. That said, a live-action Prime Video series is the biggest high fantasy show of the decade.

The project in question is The Lord of the Rings: The Rings of Power. The Prime Video high fantasy series is part of one of the most popular franchises of all time, as Tolkien's books and the Peter Jackson movie franchise have turned this universe into perhaps the go-to when the topic turns to high fantasy. The show will return next week, but changed in a way it has never been.

The Lord Of The Rings: The Rings Of Power is the biggest high fantasy series of the decade for a few reasons, with one of them being its scale. This is a show that is not afraid to spend to get the most immersive visuals or dynamic battle sequences. In fact, Prime Video's The Rings of Power is the most expensive TV show of all time, cashing out around $1 billion for the streamer's five-season plan for the high fantasy series. No other genre show has had that kind of financial backing behind it, and The Rings of Power puts it all onscreen.

Sign in to claim your place on the leaderboard!

Sign in to claim your place on the leaderboard!

Through a mix of cutting-edge VFX and practical effects, the high fantasy series fully immerses the viewer into Middle-earth, with The Lord Of The Rings: The Rings Of Power not only being a visually stunning show with grand sets, but also boasting some of the most intricate, large-scale, and impactful battle sequences ever put to the screen. Honestly, this is a show whose scope easily competes with that of high-budget movies, and it has managed to do so through 16 successful episodes thus far. The Rings Of Power holds a high 84% critics' score on Rotten Tomatoes ahead of its return.

The Lord of the Rings: The Rings of Power season 3 will debut on Prime Video on November 11 with a massive four-episode premiere. This time around, the high fantasy series has changed its release schedule. Not only is the initial binge drop longer, but The Rings of Power season 3 will then release two episodes weekly every Wednesday through November 25.

That is not the only major change for the fantasy series, which will be quite different from what fans have been used to. One of the biggest differences when it comes to season 3 is that it will pick back up with characters like Elrond, Galadriel, and Sauron after a five-year time jump. Season 3 will also be darker and with a heightened sense of urgency, given that Sauron is no longer hiding behind a persona, like he did in season 1 as Halbrand, and season 2 as Annatar, trying to forge the One Ring. The Lord of the Rings: The Rings of Power gets bigger than ever.

Ramayana Ending Explained: What Happens To Rama & How It Sets Up Part 2

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

The following contains spoilers for Ramayana: Rise of a LegendRamayana: Rise of a Legend is one of the year's big cinematic epics, with an ending that directly sets up the sequel in more than just one way. Based on the Hindu myth of the same name, Ramayana focuses on the epic battle that brews between the noble Rama and the dangerous Ravana. The first film takes its time laying out the world and setting up the conflict, with the pair not even meeting in the film.

Instead, the movie explores both characters' strengths and weaknesses to underscore what separates them on a deeply moral and human level. The film is uninterested in doing a typical 'good vs. evil' narrative, instead adding little touches of nuance to characters like Ravana, who are portrayed almost heroically in the cold open of the film as he leads his people to freedom.

Ramayana is a foundational morality tale that has endured for ages, with those same core themes present in the blockbuster film that's dividing critics. The movie is very much a part 1, leaving the stage open for next year's sequel. However, there's a lot more going on in the film's ending than just a tease for the follow-up.

Ramayana: Rise of a Legend ends on a big cliffhanger, with Rama set to venture across the realms to confront Ravana in a bid to save his wife Sita. For much of Ramayana, Rama and Ravana operate outside of one another's lives. Even when Ravana learns that Rama has been born and could pose a risk to him, he chooses restraint and patience rather than wage war.

However, the discovery that Rama and his family had defined his sister and subsequently wiped out her demon army prompts him to target the prince, capturing Sita and killing Jatayu when he tries to intervene. For the most part, the first film is laying the groundwork for the world and the characters, establishing Rama's might and showcasing his gradual growth into a man capable of fighting Ravana.

The film concludes with a deliberate tease of the next movie, with Rama learning a bit about Ravana's champions and family members that he'll have to overcome to reach the enmy king. Coupled with a furious Rama's final vow to kill Ravana and save Sita, the finale of the film is a very overt tease for the sequel, which is poised to be a far more direct and action-packed affair.

One of the interesting wrinkles of the film is the way Ramayana doesn't simply make Ravana a villain for Rama to overcome. The film takes pains to portray the character as a multilayered person, whose origin story centers around his efforts to life his people out of exile and back to the surface world. He is, in many ways, the protagonist of his own story instead of just an antagonist.

This plays into the movie's focus on the balance between order and chaos, but quietly inverts them in effective ways. Rama, as the mortal avatar of Vishnu, is tied inherently to order. However, he's also a chaotic whirlwind in battle and resists expectations to stay true to his heart. Conversely, the chaos-bringing Ravana is shown as a king, commanding a system built up in the wake of his victories.

The film isn't necessarily about good vs. evil, even when Ravana begins crossing serious moral lines within the narrative. It's a story of two forces that circle one another and contrast against each other. While Rama is content to abdicate his throne and is quick to rage when Sita is threatened, Ravana proves deceptively cruel and patient when it comes to ensuring his rule.

The thing to remember about Ramayana is that to millions of people around the world, the tale of the Ramayana is just as much a religious text as it is a mythic epic. The central story of the film is rooted in the morals and messages laid out in the original tale. Ramayana has inspired people for ages, underlining the importance of honor, loyalty, and humility through Rama's arc.

Ravana, by contrast, showcases the power that can be amassed, while laying the groundwork for his defeat. His overwhelming confidence leaves him open to being defeated in the follow-up. Ramayana: Rise of a Legend is still about those things, reinforcing its perspective on the danger of pride and hostility by having other characters like Shurpanakha and her husband Vidyutjihva suffer for theirs.

Ramayana is in many ways a morality play, highlighting the inner strength that comes from Rama and Sita that provides them with a happiness and peace that Ravana and his people have never known. Despite the power they've gained, there's still an aura of chaos in their perspective on the world that will invite chaos to their door, with a vengeful Rama in the follow-up.

Jon Bernthal’s 13-Episode Action-Packed Thriller Is a Start to Finish Masterpiece

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Jon Bernthal is one of those actors who feels tailor-made for action. Whether it's slaughtering zombies in The Walking Dead, dispatching scores of foes in The Accountant movies, or serving in the Call of Duty franchise, Bernthal has made a career out of playing hardened killers. Yet the role most people will probably remember him for is Frank Castle, aka the Punisher. Bernthal first played Castle in Season 2 of the original Daredevil and immediately won fans over by showing that Castle's hardened exterior had layers. Soon after, Bernthal led his own spinoff series, The Punisher.

When The Punisher starts, Castle has tracked down and killed the gangsters responsible for his family's deaths. However, he struggles with nightmares about losing his family and a loss of purpose. Things start heating up when a mysterious figure named "Micro" (Ebon Moss-Bachrach) reveals that Castle's family was killed due to the fallout of a mission he undertook in Afghanistan. Castle immediately starts tracking down the men behind the conspiracy, allying himself with Micro while also trying to stay one step ahead of Homeland Security agent Dinah Madani (Amber Rose Revah).

Anyone who watches The Punisher won't be surprised to learn there's plenty of bloodshed, as Castle uses his military skills and seemingly unlimited weaponry to slaughter criminals. What is surprising is the deep dive the series takes into Castle's mental state, showcasing that his war on crime is really all he has left to live for. Another tragic element is the reveal that Castle's former friend, Billy Russo (Ben Barnes), is the architect of his suffering. Marvel Comics fans will immediately recognize Russo as Jigsaw, the Punisher's heavily scarred archenemy, but Netflix's take on The Punisher fleshes him out into a compelling character—which makes his betrayal sting all the more.

This tragic element is what makes The Punisher stand out. Most people wrongly see the character as a power fantasy or write him off as a mere psychopath, but this series goes the extra mile to show that Castle's life isn't all it's cracked up to be. Years after The Punisher wrapped up its run on Netflix, Jason Aaron summed up Castle's character perfectly in the afterword to Punisher: The King of Killers — and how the Netflix series nailed that aspect.

"At the end of the day, despite the complicated nature of the character, despite the changing times and disperate viewpoints, the story of Frank Castle is fundamentally one very simple thing: a tragedy...The long, bloody tragedy of the Punisher will never be finished."

The Punisher lasted only two seasons on Netflix before being canceled, yet many fans felt Season 2 was a step down from Season 1 because of a pair of less-than-menacing antagonists and a scattershot plot. However, it wasn't the end of Jon Berenthal's time playing the character. Bernthal would return as Castle in Daredevil: Born Again, reuniting with former Punisher writer Dario Scardapane in the process. The best was yet to come, as Bernthal also co-wrote and starred in The Punisher: One Last Kill, which hews closely to The Punisher Season 1 in terms of emotional heft and action.Bernthal also plays the Punisher in Spider-Man: Brand New Day and, surprisingly, is one of the best parts of the movie. A large part of that is due to the vitriolic back-and-forth between Castle and Tom Holland's titular web-slinger, but there's also a moment that highlights Castle as being more than a lean, mean, criminal-killing machine. When he admonishes Peter Parker for choosing to stay hidden from his former girlfriend, MJ (Zendaya), Castle says, "Kid, this is a sentence." That single line highlights how tortured of a character he is, and gives the kind of depth The Punisher was aiming for.

While The Punisher had its ups and downs, it's arguably worth watching to see Jon Bernthal bring one of Marvel's most complicated characters to life. The blood and violence are just the cherry on top.
