Kristen Bell Gives Sneak Peek at What's Thawing for Frozen 3

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Kristen Bell shared an update on Frozen 3 after reprising her role as Princess Anna in the upcoming Walt Disney Animation Studios sequel.

Kristen Bell is sharing a glimpse into the unknown.

As production for Frozen 3 heats up, the Nobody Wants This actress detailed stepping back into Princess Anna’s shoes for the third installment in the beloved Disney animated film franchise.

“Honestly, I’m living my dreams. I'm really living my dreams right now,” Bell shared during an appearance on the Nov. 4 episode of The Jennifer Hudson Show. “At 46 years old, you can still live all of your dreams.”

And she also opened the door into singing what will surely be magical musical numbers.

“I recorded two songs on Friday of this past week,” Bell revealed to Jennifer Hudson. “I love them so much.”

Though fans will have to try to let go of any impatience since the third film won’t be hitting theaters until Nov. 24, 2027, the Veronica Mars alum looks forward to the finished product.

“I just am very proud of what's being written,” Bell shared, “and I'm very excited to share it with the world.”

And while it may seem like this is the first time in forever since there has been an update on Frozen 3, Bell and her costar Idina Menzel—who voices Elsa—recently shared how they made sure magic would flow once again for a third film immediately after Frozen 2 was released in 2019.

“The second [Frozen 2] was over I was waiting by the phone and/or texting everyone, ‘When are we gonna do it?’” Bell told E! News in August. “And so I was in an anticipatory place.”

But Menzel emphasized that the Mouse House is always intentional with its moves and will “wait for the right time.”

“They're super patient with it,” she shared. “I guess we were a little impatient because we really like to get back to the process. We love being together. We love revisiting these characters.”

As for what more fans can expect when it’s time to build a snowman once more? Disney Animation head Jared Bush proved that some things may never truly change as Frozen 3 will tell “a very special story” like its predecessors.

That said, he teased to Collider in an interview published Nov. 3 the tale is “one that will surprise people.”

Until Frozen 3 soars into theaters next year, don’t hold your excitement back anymore and read for Frozen secrets…

1. An adaptation of Hans Christian Andersen's 1845 fairy tale The Snow Queen, Frozen was actually in development at Disney for almost a decade (and was briefly considered for a hand-drawn animated feature back in 1937).

The first modern attempt happened in 2002, and when 2003 also proved unsuccessful, more stalled attempts followed over the years. Frozen really didn't start coming together until Christopher Buck, who directed Tarzan, came aboard in 2008, but the film wasn't commissioned until 2011 following Tangled's box office success.

And in 2012, Jennifer Lee, who initially came on as a screenwriter, was named co-director, making her the first woman to direct an animated film by Walt Disney Animation Studios.

2. The movie was going to be titled The Snow Queen after the original fairy tale, but the filmmakers decided to change it to Frozen "because, to us, it represents the movie. Frozen plays on the level of ice and snow but also the frozen relationship, the frozen heart that has to be thawed," producer Peter Del Vecho explained to Bleeding Cool, denying it was inspired by Tangled. (The Snow Queen title was still used overseas.)

3. Idina Menzel and Kristen Bell auditioned and met with the producers of Tangled for the role of Rapunzel, which ultimately went to Mandy Moore. (Santino Fontana, who gave life to Anna's temporary fiancé Hans, originally auditioned for the role of Flynn in the 2010 film as well).

Bell, whose dream since she was a little girl was to voice a Disney character, was the "very first actress" to audition for Anna, with Lee saying, "We did audition a lot of people for that but she was the first and the best. We just fell in love with her."

4. Menzel's audition for Elsa, meanwhile, was actually a table-read with Bell before both had been officially cast, according to Lee.

"They read the whole script and at the end—we didn't have any songs written yet—and what they did was sing a duet and they sung 'Wind Beneath My Wings,' but they sung it like sisters and what you mean to me," she explained. "And there wasn't a dry eye in the house after they sang...that is when they knew, that was the whole potential and understanding the power of music and of what this emotional story can be."

5. In the early versions of the project, Elsa was actually the villain of the story (with one of the early concept art designs being inspired by Bette Midler and her "showgirl stage presence"), according to former Disney animation designer Claire Keane, who released the image on her web site.

"Originally, she was much more villainous," Buck allowed to Nerdist. "It's very easy to go in that direction for that type of character, but as the time went and we evolved the story, she became much more three-dimensional. Before, I thought she was one-dimensional but a very fully fleshed out character."

The moment the character really changed, though, was after they watched earlier cuts and realized it wasn't exactly working: "Someone came up with the brilliant idea...'what if the protagonist, Anna, and Elsa were related?'" art director Michael Giaimo revealed. "Because they weren't always siblings, they weren't always related, and that was the beginning of finding these characters."

6. The hardest character for the Frozen team to nail down aside from Elsa was actually Olaf, who was originally then-villainous Elsa's first guard.

"She created these snowman guards and Marshmallow was kind of a holdover from that because he was this scary snowman guard. Olaf was the very first guard she created," Buck explained, adding he was still funny at that time.

After changing the character of Elsa completely, it wasn't until the directors first sat down with Josh Gad that they found the character. "Josh adlibbed the line 'it's like a baby unicorn' and there was something about that innocence," Del Vecho told Kidz World.

7. One main character who wasn't originally in the movie was Hans, Anna's suitor-turned-villain, with Giaimo explaining, "Hans was never originally in the story in older versions...Kristoff always was in the story or a version of the Kristoff character and Anna. And then Hans was brought in at one point but he wasn't always a villain who turned initially, and then someone came up with the idea of him turning. He was always potentially a villain, but we gave it away much sooner."

8. Anna's oft-repeated "Wait what?" catchphrase? An ad-lib by Bell. She also slipped in one slightly off-color joke. "We slid it under the radar," she detailed to Vanity Fair of Anna's singing, "For years I've roamed these empty halls / Why have a ballroom with no balls?" The NSFW lyric almost got snipped, she admitted, "But then we were like, 'What are you talking about? That’s not what it means. Don’t be a perv.'"

11. After having a few of the first songs they originally composed for the film not make it in, songwriting couple Robert Lopez and Kristen Anderson-Lopez were feeling a little low, so they went for walk in Brooklyn's Prospect Park.

Listening to songs by Adele and Avril Lavigne and being surrounded by nature, Anderson-Lopez told Playbill, "We were in this kind of emo place of imagining what it would be like to be this perfect person who messed up once, and was chased away from everything she has known and all the people she has been sacrificing for."

Within a day, they would write the film's most iconic song that would go on to sell over 10 million copies in 2014 and win the Oscar for Best Original Song. All together now, "Let it go, let it goooooooo."

12. And after hearing "Let It Go" for the first time, Lee said she knew they had to rework the character of Elsa and her motivations completely.

"Half of us were crying. And then I just went, 'I have to rewrite the whole movie,'" she said on the Scriptnotes podcast. "I really, it was—I was just like I'm going to go lie down for a couple minutes. But it was the best thing. We knew we had the movie."

13. On the same podcast, Lee admitted there was "a lot of push and pull" when it came to Elsa's look after her transformation via "Let It Go" and how sexy to make her after this awakening.

"Everyone was seduced by her. And so there was this tug of war I think, a bit, of letting people have a little—people who wanted to have that a little and not be afraid of it, but not make it a sexual statement," the co-director explained. "It's more a moment of, for me, it was like you strut and you say nobody is looking, this is what I'm going to—I'm not going to be afraid of my sexuality. I'm not going to be afraid of who I am. I'm not going to be afraid of anything about myself."

14. After eagle-eyed fans spotted one of many easter eggs in the film—Tangled's Rapunzel (post haircut) and Eugene briefly appearing in a scene during the morning of Elsa's coronation—a compelling fan theory that tied together Frozen, Tangled and Little Mermaid surfaced: Anna and Elsa's parents died when their boat sank...what if it was when they were on their way to the Tangled pair's wedding? And what if the sunken ship in The Little Mermaid is the ship they were sailing on?!

15. This theory only works if you believe Anna and Elsa's parents actually did die in the shipwreck, something Buck speculated about in a Reddit AMA, saying, in his mind, the King and Queen of Arendelle, as well as their "baby boy," did not actually die after being shipwrecked. (Who knew they had a son?)

He said they washed up on the shore of a jungle and built "a tree house" before being "eaten by a leopard." That baby boy? Tarzan, of course.

"That's my fun little world," he later told MTV. While the theory isn't official (and definitely takes a hit when you factor in the events of Frozen II), Buck said, "Whatever people want to believe, go for it. If you want to tie them all together, then do it."

16. The original ending of the film was drastically different, with the earlier versions, of course, presenting Elsa as the villain and not having Anna and Elsa related.

Beginning with a prophecy about "a ruler with a frozen heart will bring destruction to the kingdom of Arendelle," viewers would learn Elsa became the Snow Queen after she was left at the altar and decided to freeze her own heart, according to EW.

By the end of the film, Anna convinces Elsa to use her powers to save Arendelle from an avalanche started by Hans, with the ultimate reveal being the prophecy was actually about the two-faced prince. (Elsa's heart would then unfreeze, making her capable of love again.)

"The problem was that we felt like we had seen it before," Del Vecho told EW of the original ending. "It wasn't satisfying. We had no emotional connection to Elsa—we didn't care about her because she had spent the whole movie being the villain. We weren't drawn in. The characters weren't relatable."

Warner Bros.' Adaptation Of Fantasy Franchise Lunar Chronicles Gets Update

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Warner Bros. is developing a new animated movie based on a fantasy novel series, and the latest update reveals the studio's bold plans for the future.

Over the years, Warner Bros. has released numerous animated flicks like The Looney Tunes, The Iron Giant, and Teen Titans Go! To the Movies. In recent years, there's been the Lord of the Rings movie The War of the Rohirrim and The Day the Earth Blew Up, along with direct-to-video releases for Scooby-Doo, Tom and Jerry, and DC Comics.

Now Warner Bros. Pictures Animation is working on an adaptation of Melissa Meyer's fantasy/sci-fi book series The Lunar Chronicles. The project was first announced three years ago, and The Wrap now has a majorupdate.

Apparently, the studio has grand plans for the future of The Lunar Chronicles, to the point where executives are reportedly planning for the film to be the first installment in a franchise. This shows that Skydance has faith in the property, even after Paramount acquired Warner Bros.

Noëlle Raffaele, who has worked as a storyboard artist on The Lego Ninjago Movie, Scoob!, Ms. Marvel and The Marvels, is directing The Lunar Chronicles adaptation. Industrial Light & Magic is creating the animation.

The Lunar Chronicles reimagines classic fairytale characters with a futuristic twist. The first book, Cinder, came out in 2012 and tells a Cinderella-like story, but the protagonist is a cyborg. Meyer followed that up with Scarlet (inspired by Little Red Riding Hood), Cress (a new take on Rapunzel), Fairest (about Snow White's Evil Queen), Winter (Snow White), the short story collection Stars Above and the Wires, and the Nerve graphic novels.

Meyer has also written several other series, including Renegades, Fortuna Beach and Gilded, and co-wrote the novels Let It Glow, The Escape Game and The Traitor Game.

Multiple animated films have performed really well this year, with Toy Story 5 currently the third-highest-grossing movie of 2026 at $1.15 billion. The Super Mario Galaxy Movie ranks fifth with $1.01 billion, while Minions & Monsters is ninth with $529 million.

Sign in to claim your place on the leaderboard!

Ice Age: Boiling Point, The Bluey Movie, Pixar's Gatto, Shrek 5, Spider-Man: Beyond the Spider-Verse, The New Simpsons Movie, The Haunting of Hotel Transylvania, and Frozen 3 are all animated movies coming out in 2027. Then 2028 will see the arrivals of Shrek spinoff Donkey and Incredibles 3.

Many of these are based on existing properties, and some, like Shrek and Frozen, lean into the fairy-tale genre, so The Lunar Chronicles is in good company when Warner Bros. releases it in a couple of years from now.

The Lunar Chronicles will be released in theaters on Friday, November 3, 2028.

Matthew Lillard’s Shaggy Return In New Scooby-Doo Series Is 2027’s Most Anticipated Comeback

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Matthew Lillard is officially returning as Shaggy in a new Scooby-Doo series arriving in 2027, marking what may be the most anticipated actor comeback of the year, if not the decade. For 2000s kids, this is nothing short of a dream come true, while also giving a whole new generation the chance to discover why Lillard's portrayal of the iconic character is so beloved.

As a 2000s kid, I grew up with the live-action Scooby-Doo (2002) and Scooby-Doo 2: Monsters Unleashed (2004) movies. In fact, the VHS tapes for these two movies were easily among the most loved and frequently watched in my collection, seeing hundreds, if not thousands, of playbacks over the years.

As a result, Matthew Lillard became the face and voice of "my Shaggy," just as he did for hundreds of thousands of kids worldwide. To this day, it's truly impossible for me to imagine another actor in the role who could even come close.

Teenage Mutant Ninja Turtles' latest series has already delivered one of the coolest redesigns of a classic villain.

Now, 22 years after Monsters Unleashed, Lillard is reprising this legendary role, albeit with a twist. Rather than returning for another live-action project, Lillard will voice Shaggy Rogers in the upcoming series Yokoso, Scooby-Doo!, marking the franchise's first original anime series. And while this isn't quite the R-rated live-action return some fans may have hoped for, I couldn't be more excited.

Yokoso, Scooby-Doo! will be a Japan-set, anime-inspired mystery adventure that pairs Scooby-Doo and Shaggy with several new characters. Notably, in addition to Lillard returning as the voice of Shaggy, longtime Scooby-Doo voice actor Frank Welker will also reprise his role as Scooby-Doo. While the franchise has seen countless animated series reboots over the years, fans can expect Yokoso, Scooby-Doo! to offer something wholly unique.

In addition to marking the franchise's first venture into anime, the series will see classic Scooby-Doo shenanigans collide with Japanese folklore, delivering a completely new setting and adventure unlike anything fans are used to. Produced by Japanese animation studio OLM and distributed by Tubi, Yokoso, Scooby-Doo! has yet to confirm an exact release date but is expected to arrive sometime in 2027, meaning Lillard's return is just around the corner.

While Lillard's Shaggy and Welker's Scooby-Doo will understandably be the stars of the show, they'll also be joined by several new characters, including Yume, a girl with magical powers; Takumi, a gadget whiz; and Scooby-Doo's uncle, Daisuke-Doo (We can only hope that Daisuke-Doo is less nefarious than Scooby's nephew, Scrappy-Doo).

Die-hard Scooby-Doo fans already know what to expect from Lillard's voice acting, as he took over as Shaggy's official animated voice around 2010, after original voice actor Casey Kasem retired from the role. Since then, Lillard has voiced Shaggy in numerous animated films, TV series, and video games. However, Yokoso, Scooby-Doo! will mark his first time venturing into anime as Shaggy Rogers, making this latest return particularly notable.

The kids who grew up watching Matthew Lillard's Scooby-Doo movies are now all grown up, and it feels like the perfect time for the actor and his co-stars, Freddie Prinze Jr., Sarah Michelle Gellar, and Linda Cardellini, to reprise their live-action roles. An R-rated movie bringing back the Mystery Inc. gang for a more mature, all-grown-up adventure could be exactly what longtime fans have been waiting for.

Matthew Lillard is already a horror icon thanks to his roles in some of the most legendary horror franchises of all time, including William Afton in Five Nights at Freddy's and Stu Macher in Scream (1996), one of the original Ghostface killers alongside Billy Loomis. Fans of both franchises would undoubtedly go wild if news broke that Lillard's horror and Scooby-Doo legacies were set to collide, especially if his fellow co-stars returned alongside him.

If you grew up a Scooby-Doo kid, let us know in the comments: Would you also like to see Matthew Lillard return for an R-rated Scooby-Doo movie?

Jennifer Lawrence and Kim Kardashian’s NYC Night Out Adds to Their Quirky 11-Year History

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Jennifer Lawrence and Kim Kardashian enjoyed a night out together in New York City yesterday, beginning the evening in regular attire and then ending it in matching Skims pajamas and robes as they left the Four Seasons hotel. At first glance, the pairing might seem unexpected, but Lawrence’s history with the Kardashian family goes back for over 10 years.

In 2023, Lawrence told Kylie Jenner while interviewing her for Interview magazine that she started following the family’s original reality show Keeping Up With the Kardashians when she was a teenager: “I’ve been watching the show since I was, not to date myself, 14, so I would say I know everything about your family,” she shared.

Her first big public moment with the Kardashian-Jenner family was in 2015. Kardashian’s mother, Kris Jenner, posted a photo of Lawrence in bed with her along with a birthday message.

In November 2017, Lawrence interviewed Kardashian on Jimmy Kimmel Live! while serving as a guest host for Jimmy Kimmel.

Kardashian revealed where they first met. “We were at a hotel in New York, and I don’t know where we were coming from, but it was like, 8, and we were going up to see this Axel Vervoordt suite at the Greenwich [Hotel] and I’m getting in the elevator with Kanye [West] and the doors are about to close and I hear Jennifer screaming, ‘I love your show!’ so loud across the whole lobby,” she recalled.

They also discussed Lawrence having dinner at Kris’s house two weeks before their interview and Kardashian dressing her up.

It marked their first significant public conversation:

Less than a year later, Lawrence quipped during a February 2018 event at The Wing that her friendship with Kardashian doesn’t go both ways. “I don’t know that she’d call me her friend,” Lawrence said. “It’s probably a one-sided friendship.”

She recalled learning she’d interview Kardashian on Kimmel’s show. “When I found out that I was going to be interviewing her, I was like, ‘Oh my God, my whole life has been building up to this moment,’” she said. “I wanted to get as much information as I could. I wanted to do my due diligence on Kim Kardashian. I went over to Kris [Jenner’s] house and had dinner with the whole family.”

Of the family generally, Lawrence said, “They are smart, grounded and normal and funny. They’re very nice people, [they’re] very close, [they have a] loyal bond with one another, which I think is a positive thing to put out into the world. They’ve been absolutely lovely to me.”

Lawrence has remained close to the Kardashian-Jenner family in the years since. (Kris even attended Lawrence’s wedding to Cooke Maroney in October 2019.)

During her 2023 conversation with Kylie, Lawrence even jokingly referred to Kris as her mother while asking a question about Kylie’s relationship with her older sister Kendall Jenner. “You and me are Leos,” she told Kylie. “We’re happy, excited golden retrievers. And Kendall, of course, is a Scorpio like our mother, Kris.” She and Kylie ended their chat by exchanging I love yous.

Last month, Lawrence was mentioned on The Kardashians, with Kardashian revealing the actress had asked for Kourtney Kardashian’s contact information to say sorry for a remark she made during a Vanity Fair interview about Kourtney.

Kardashian said that Lawrence wrote in her apology email, “I just feel like I’m one of your sisters.”

An investigation regarding the Jalen Hurts situation has not begun

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

The return of Eagles quarterback Jalen Hurts to the team’s facility on Wednesday is a reminder that plenty of questions remain regarding the decisions made as to his health before flying to London with a blood clot in his leg, playing in London with a blood clot in his leg, and flying back from London with a blood clot in his leg.

A widespread presumption has emerged that the NFL and NFL Players Association have commenced a joint investigation into the care and recommendations Hurts received from team and league doctors. However, the investigation has yet to start.

The statement issued by the league and the NFLPA said only that they are “working together to review the matter.” A formal investigation has not yet begun, of any kind. The union has the right under the Collective Bargaining Agreement to initiate an investigation. That hasn’t happened, either.

If the NFLPA will be working with the NFL in any official investigation, it’s critical that the union insist on a process that will be fair, objective, and transparent. It can’t be a CYA exercise.

Basically, it can’t give off the same vibes that have emanated from the team and the league, to date. The union can’t afford to create the impression that it’s complicit in a coverup. And the process needs to lead to real change, aimed at ensuring that someone disconnected from the team (and possibly from the league, especially as to international games) will be involved in medical decisions as to potentially serious health situations.

The NFL and the NFLPA already employ an unaffiliated doctor when it comes to diagnosing concussions and clearing players to return after suffering concussions. Other health conditions require a similar layer of detachment, in order to ensure that the shared incentives of the team and the player for the player to play will not override the importance of treating the player like a patient.

Levon Hawke to Star With Anthony Hopkins in Indie Drama ‘Twenty-Seven’ (EXCLUSIVE)

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Levon Hawke (“Blink Twice,” “The Crowded Room”) will star opposite Academy Award winner Anthony Hopkins (“The Father”) in the drama “Twenty-Seven.”

In the film, written and directed by Gregory Caruso, Hawke portrays a 27-year-old aspiring screenwriter attempting to manage his relationships and his OCD while struggling to break into the film industry. Hopkins plays his friend and mentor, an 80-year-old television actor-turned-theater professor, who is also fighting Parkinson’s disease. Related Stories Abdul El-Sayed Has Heated Exchange With Sky News Reporter Texas' New Attorney General Nathan Johnson Composed the Music for 67 'Dragon Ball Z' Episodes, Years Before Election Day

“I wrote this character hoping someone like Anthony Hopkins would play him. I never imagined it would actually be Anthony Hopkins,” said Caruso. “He brings a mastery to the role I still can’t fully describe, and Levon meets him in every scene with real vulnerability and intelligence. At its heart, ‘Twenty-Seven’ is about the uncertainty of growing up, feeling stuck between where you are and where you want to be, and the people who help you find your way.”

“Twenty-Seven,” from Caruso’s Bristol Pictures, is currently in production in Los Angeles. David Worthen Brooks (“Misty Green,” “Appendage”) will produce alongside Dustin Cook (“Flock of Four,” “The Frontier”).

Caruso’s crew includes director of photography Matthew Pothier (“Mutt”), production designer Emma Rose Mead (“To Leslie”), costume designer Jenny Eagan (“Knives Out”), hair and makeup leads Mandy Artusato and Gina Marie DeAngelis (“One Battle After Another”), editor Aleshka Ferrero (“Euphoria”) and casting director Vickie Thomas (“Michael,” “Misty Green”).

The project marks the first lead role for Hawke, an actor and composer who is the son of Ethan Hawke and Uma Thurman and the younger brother of actress Maya Hawke. Levon made his feature film debut in Zoë Kravitz’ 2024 thriller “Blink Twice” and appeared in the Apple TV series “The Crowded Room.” He then joined the Peter Dinklage-starring western “The Thicket” and appeared in the indie film “Wildcat,” directed by his father and starring his sister. In 2024, Hawke made his directorial debut with the play “Picture Day,” followed by roles in Josh Safdie’s Oscar-nominated film “Marty Supreme” and Hayley Kiyoko’s coming-of-age movie “Girls Like Girls.” The rising star will next be seen in A24’s “Peaked” and on season two of the acclaimed Prime Video series “Mr. & Mrs. Smith.” Hawke is represented by CAA and HJTH.

Two-time Oscar winner Hopkins’ legendary career is marked by such classics as “The Silence of the Lambs,” “Bram Stoker’s Dracula,” “The Remains of the Day,” “Nixon,” “Amistad” and “Meet Joe Black.” Hopkins recently released his debut album “Life is a Dream” and has several acting projects in the works, including D.J. Caruso’s “A Visit to Grandpa’s,” Richard Eyre’s “Housekeeper” and Guy Ritchie’s “Wife & Dog.” Hopkins is represented by UTA and GGSSC.

Matt Damon Reveals Wife Suffered Brain Aneurysm Weeks Before 2024 Oscars

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Matt Damon revealed in a new interview that his wife suffered a brain aneurysm weeks before the 2024 Oscars.

Reflecting on the 20th anniversary of The Departed in a GQ interview, Damon — who was not nominated for that Martin Scorsese film — said he wanted attend the Academy Awards in 2007 to witness Scorsese get his first Oscar, but he was shooting The Bourne Ultimatum. Similarly, with Oppenheimer favored at the 2024 Oscars, Damon was eager to join the cast and crew of the Christopher Nolan film at the ceremony.

However, a week before those Oscars, “We had this whole scary thing happen where my wife had a brain aneurysm,” Damon revealed. “She’s totally fine, thank God, but [she] had this brain surgery the week before the Oscars.”

The actor has been married to his wife Luciana since 2005, and they share three children together. A week after Luciana’s surgery, the Damons attended a Vanity Fair post-Oscars party to celebrate Oppenheimer’s Best Picture win, one of seven Academy Awards the film took home. Kim Kardashian’s Morning Show Look Is a Vintage Paco Rabanne Pastille Party Dress

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Good morning, Kim Kardashian! Starting off a day of press appointments in New York City, the reality star-turned-actor joined her fellow All’s Fair costars on Good Morning America to promote the legal drama’s second season. And while most of us need a cup of coffee (or two) to get ourselves dressed and out the door, she woke up and slipped straight into a vintage Paco Rabanne party dress.

The look, styled by Law Roach, was an intricate peach gown pulled from the Spring/Summer 1989 couture collection. The top was crafted from hundreds of metal discs, which were stitched together to create a one-shoulder silhouette with a side cutout, making for an asymmetrical, backless piece of armor. From the waist, a long silk-crepe skirt floated to the ankle, where it was met by little heeled sandals with clear PVC straps.

The piece of history was sourced by Maison Vintage Virtuoso, a Paris-based archival studio focused on couture, and was reportedly introduced to Kardashian’s team four months ago. (The same ensemble recently sold for nearly $11,500 on 1st Dibs.)

On GMA, Kardashian shared several candid stories from her time filming All’s Fair, and even revealed that she thought Sarah Paulson’s lower-register on-camera voice was her real voice until just a few weeks ago. Despite this confession, the costars were some of the closest on set, and often made one another break character. “They made Sarah and I one time get up and take a walk around the building because they said we needed to get it together,” the Skims founder told the talk show. “We were laughing so hard.”

Kardashian, who later changed into a sheer knit minidress from Dolce and Gabbana’s Autumn/Winter 1997 collection, has curated a press tour wardrobe full of custom gowns (Ashi Studio), vintage pieces (Todd Oldham), and standout couture looks (Schiaparelli).

‘Wild Horse Nine’ Review: Martin McDonagh’s Prickly but Unconvincing CIA Satire Recasts ‘In Bruges’ as the Stuff of a Classic Western

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

[Editor’s note: This review was originally published on September 3, 2026 during the 2026 Venice Film Festival. Searchlight Pictures will release it in theaters on Friday, November 6.]

Another melancholically splenetic Martin McDonagh jag about a banter-mad pair of murderers who go on a boys trip to purgatory (where they search their souls as they wait for death to find them), the prickly but unconvincing “Wild Horse Nine” strains to translate the post-Guy Ritchie bark of “In Bruges” into the language of a classic American Western. In this case, an American Western set on Easter Island and backdropped by Augusto Pinochet’s rise to power in the fall of 1973.  Related Stories ‘North of North’ Season 2 Review: Netflix’s Defiantly Nice Arctic Sitcom Is for the Warmhearted F**k-Ups ‘Hen’ Review: A Cockeyed Comedy Sees Europe’s Migrant Crisis Through the Eyes of a Chicken

McDonagh’s decision to recast his same old script against the backdrop of such a real and terrible event grants this new telling a welcome sense of scale, both in terms of its characters’ morality, as well as in regards to their greater place in history. But that decision is a double-edged sword that cuts the film off at the knees. Where “In Bruges” kicked off with the killing of a fictional altar boy, “Wild Horse Nine” is overshadowed by the deaths of thousands upon thousands of very real people, and that reality bleeds into the edges of the frame even when the seriocomic absurdity of McDonagh’s writing demands a world of pure invention.

Does the overlap make “Wild Horse Nine” feel glib? Yes, and thank god for that — if not for glibness the movie would lose the majority of its few solid laughs. The problem is that it also makes “Wild Horse Nine” feel as transparently manufactured as the CIA-backed overthrow of a foreign democracy. I can accept that an inexperienced hitman might have a dark night of the soul after shooting an innocent child during confession. By contrast, I struggle to believe that a veteran CIA agent who openly brags about his involvement in the Kennedy assassination would have a come-to-Jesus moment after orchestrating the demise of Salvador Allende. McDonagh has some good fun wrestling with whether or not we should care about such a mottled change of heart, but the point is gradually rendered moot in a movie whose emotions are less credible than its conspiracies.

The original Company man, Chris (John Malkovich, more locked in than he’s been on screen since “Burn After Reading”) is a natural hinge for the off-kilter comedy that McDonagh builds around him, in that he can be a joy to watch even when you don’t buy a word he’s saying. More than just an ordinary CIA agent, he’s the living personification of America’s efforts to destabilize foreign governments across the 20th century. He was close with Patrice Lumumba until the day the Congolese leader was executed by a firing squad. He was in Cuba to spur on the exiles who failed to overthrow Fidel Castro in 1961. He was even in Dallas, USA on a Friday afternoon in November 1963, doing things his ornery boss MJ (Steve Buscemi) would much prefer he didn’t tell you about.

He traveled to all of those places, and got all of those people killed, with the blithe imperiousness of a flag waving in the wind. Chris says that he’s killed 68 people over the course of his career, and he offers that information as easily as a rumor — in a way that makes it sound like the number could be half that, or double, and it wouldn’t make a difference.

Recently, fate has blown Chris and his office husband/platonic life partner/exasperated straightman Lee (McDonagh vet Sam Rockwell) down south to Chile, where the two of them have been hard at work orchestrating the September 11th coup against the democratically elected socialist president, knowing full well that it would plunge the country into decades of brutal dictatorship. Chris appears to delight in sowing chaos, and the fact that he gets to wear a cute little Panama hat while he does it only boosts his joie de vivre.

But Chris is fraying at the seams in ways that we can’t see at first — ways that will only begin to reveal themselves when he and Lee decide to celebrate their imminent success with a sightseeing trip to Easter Island, a short 2,300 miles off the Western coast of Santiago. There are still a couple of months to go before the coup, but the wheels are in motion, and there’s little for these men to do for now besides fly to Rapa Nui, look at the “big heads,” flirt with a couple of beautiful young leftists (the prissier but gentler of whom is played by “Ema” mega-talent Mariana di Girolamo), and mordantly reflect upon their role in the awfulness Pinochet will soon inflict upon his own people. That task is complicated and confused by the fact that Chris seems to be losing his memory, which casts Lee in the role of his peevish but loving caretaker.

“Wild Horse Nine” is only the second feature that’s ever been shot on the ultra-remote soil of Rapa Nui (Kevin Reynolds’ 1994 epic “Rapa Nui” is credited as the first), and McDonagh rightly exalts in the fact that he’s brought Hollywood to the end of the world. The first half of his movie largely adheres to the fiction that Chris and Lee are just on Easter Island to fuck around, which offers a semi-relaxed opportunity for us to take in the sights. Ben Davis’ sun-bleached cinematography renders the hills and beaches of Rapa Nui with the veneer of a lost idyll, the ancient moai statues assuming an especially sacred charge when contrasted against such profane characters.

Chris bemusedly laughs the statues off with ethnographic glee, mocking one of the shorter figures as a dwarf (in case the “In Bruges” déjà vu wasn’t already strong enough), but it’s clear that he’s shaken by the fact that some of them almost 750 years old. That they’ve stood there in silent tribute to Polynesia’s ancestors as empires rose and fell on faraway shores, each of them a graveyard of forgotten people just like him. By the same token, it’s similarly clear that he’s trying to get at something in his own mind as he and Lee listen to Judy Collins in their rental car, argue over whether or not any Nazis made it into heaven (among other even more aggressively screen-written subjects), and whisper sweet nothings to the wild horses that run about the island, but the specific nature of that something is hard for him to pin down.

Malkovich has the time of his life with that search for Chris’ last speck of humanity, which shines up from the bottom of his soul as his mind continues to erode. At once both insidiously relaxed and also choked with Catholic guilt, Malkovich’s performance marries the nonchalance of a cowboy to the paranoia of a bounty target. One minute he’s waxing poetic about dinosaurs, and the next he’s rigging his luggage with explosives (maybe) and checking the “Memento”-like reminder notes he’s slipped into his jacket pocket.

“Wild Horse Nine” loves Chris too much to pretend like we’re not supposed to, but Malkovich never asks for our sympathy because he knows that he’ll get it anyway, and not just because the purpose of Rockwell’s character is to ensure that we do. It’s Chris’ job to make rooting for the bad guys feel like the right choice. It’s the cornerstone of America’s post-war foreign policy.

So while Chris might be cut from the same cloth as John Wayne (to the extent that Monument Valley is revealed to be a place of great importance to him), his arc is aimed in the opposite direction of the one that Ethan Edwards traces across “The Searchers”; Chris is a major asshole this movie gradually puckers into a wayward hero, and while it’s all too easy to root for him to complete that transformation before time runs out, at no point does “Wild Horse Nine” sell us on the premise that it would mean anything in the end — even to him.

The movie, blessedly, isn’t Catholic enough to offer Chris a big moment of divine expiation; in lieu of building to a grand confession, he and Lee simply run afoul of their CIA higher-ups until their PTO turns into a matter of American national security. Guns are fired. Hostages are taken. The word “fuck” is used like a punctuation mark, and regrets are muttered into the wind long after there’s any way for them to matter.

It all gets a bit too busy and madcap as “Wild Horse Nine” — freighted with sadness during even its cheekiest bits, as is McDonagh’s way — scrambles to make up for the general listlessness of its first half. Never as funny as it is sardonic, and always reaching so hard to be more of both, the film’s most successful moments are mined from the ambient tension between memory and erasure.

Chris, we begin to suspect, would do anything to forget what he’s done, and his own mind is graciously willing to get him halfway there. The rest of the CIA will do anything it takes to make sure that Chris doesn’t share whatever it is that he still remembers. Surrounding them on all sides are statues that predated America by several hundred years, and will likely continue to watch over Rapa Nui long after our empire has been wiped away like a tear in the rain. In “Wild Horse Nine,” American interventionism is less effective as the set-up for some jokes than it is as their punchline.

“Wild Horse Nine” premiered at the 2026 Venice Film Festival. Searchlight Pictures will release it in theaters on Friday, November 6.

There's Demand to Remake Final Fantasy 6 from Within Square Enix, Says Series Boss

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Could a remake of Final Fantasy 6 happen at some point? The idea's been floating around for a while, but we're now, apparently, at a point where Square Enix employees want to see it.

This is according to series veteran and current brand manager Yoshinori Kitase, who specifically notes that there's an internal demand for said remake while speaking to IGN.

The outlet asks Kitase for his thoughts on additional remake projects now that Final Fantasy 7 Remake's trilogy is coming to a close, and while he certainly doesn't commit to a definitive answer, his mention of Final Fantasy 6 is enough to raise eyebrows.

Kitase says that while he personally doesn't "have any particular feeling towards any kind of remake" at this moment in time, and that he doesn't believe he'll work on one "anytime soon", there's demand "inside the company".

"Inside the company and also among the Final Fantasy fans, there's been many people asking for a remake of Final Fantasy VI, so who knows?," he comments.

To me, this seems like the kind of seed that could sprout into something down the line. It's easy to forget that companies as big as Square Enix are seeing constant churn in terms of development teams and project leads; all it takes is for someone to gather support and pitch this potential remake at the right time.

Interestingly, though, it does sound like Kitase's priorities lie elsewhere. At the end of the interview, he says that he'd like to spearhead something with a focus on "battle action", but "without losing the characteristics of a Final Fantasy story".

Jared Verse was upset that Browns teammates didn't react when he took a cheap shot

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Browns defensive end Jared Verse was picked up and thrown to the ground by Steelers guard Mason McCormick on Sunday, and McCormick wasn’t the only person Verse was upset with afterward.

Verse told reporters in Cleveland today that he was upset both by McCormick’s cheap shot and by his teammates’ reaction, or lack thereof. Verse thought the rest of the Browns’ players should have had his back, and didn’t.

“To be quite honest with you, that was the play that made me really angry,” Verse said. “I was already upset about the game, the way things were moving, but at that point it became deep. You’re doing dirty shit, you’re trying to sweep my legs out. That honestly could have put me in a position to not be able to finish the season, put me in a position to change my life in a very bad aspect, so that upset me. The lack of reaction by those around me had upset me a lot. They were focused on the game and everything like that, but a similar play happened to Derek Barnett on the next field goal, and I spazzed, I lost it. Because those are my brothers, those are my teammates, and when you do something like that to them, it upsets me deeply. We have to get better, we have to be aware. These are my teammates, these are my brothers, and we’re not fighting for each other.”

Verse said he addressed his feelings with his teammates. Verse did acknowledge that he brought some of it on himself by the trash talk he was directing at the Steelers, but he still felt that his teammates should have protected him when it escalated from verbal to physical.

“That’s a conversation that we’ve had, we’ve spoken about,” Verse said. “I made my concerns known, I’ve made my issues known about the situation, but also, I didn’t put them in a position to let them know that, I didn’t let them know beforehand I was going to talk to the Steelers and Steeler Nation. I’ve got to be better.”

Verse, the 2024 NFL Defensive Rookie of the Year who arrived in Cleveland in the trade that sent Myles Garrett to the Rams, has been a disappointment this season. His teammates may be wishing he’d focus a little less on talking trash and getting into altercations on the field, and a little more on sacking the opposing quarterback — something he hasn’t done yet this season.

PS6 Development on Track, Sony Making 'Solid Progress' Towards Next-Gen

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Work on the PS6 and the next console generation as a whole is currently "progressing as scheduled" at PlayStation, it's been confirmed.

Speaking alongside the release of Sony's latest quarterly financial results, CFO Lin Tao said the company is on track to deliver its next PlayStation system, and it intends to make "solid progress" towards the next generation within the next fiscal year (via VGC).

The quote from Tao reads: "Looking ahead to the next fiscal year, we aim to continue to enhance user engagement and further strengthen monetisation through the release of strong titles and the expansion of our service. We also intend to make solid progress in preparing for the next-generation platform."

Tao continued by talking up "investment in the new platform" from the start of the next fiscal year. It has to attach "great importance" to a new console and "make the necessary investments" for it. "We have to support the soundness of our platform and think about how rationally we can invest in that platform. We will announce that at an appropriate time in the future."

A few months ago, Sony CEO Hiroki Totoki claimed the firm hadn't yet decided when the PS6 will come out. Consistent rumours point to a release in the next few years, however, with a potential $1,000+ price point and a handheld device to go with it.

With the prices of components still on the rise — which Sony would need to manufacture PS6 hardware — the potential cost of a next-gen machine looks like it'll be the biggest factor in whether PlayStation fans jump on board or not. When we polled Push Square users, 65% of you said you wouldn't pay four figures for a video game console.

Microsoft will also have a new Xbox to launch in the coming years, currently codenamed Xbox Helix, and that's suggested to cost as much as $1,800, depending on whether it decides to subsidise the box. PS6 is at least rumoured to be cheaper to make than Xbox Helix, so the cost shouldn't get quite that high.

Elsewhere in its financial report, Sony confirmed PS5 sales have reached 97.9 million. With a boost from GTA 6, it should hit the 100 million sales milestone by the end of the next quarter, and become the fourth PlayStation system to do so.

The 10 Greatest Cozy Fantasy Books of All Time, Ranked

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

The term "cozy fantasy" as a recognized, distinct subgenre of fantasy was only coined around 2020, mostly driven by the desire for emotional escapism and comfy entertainment during the COVID-19 pandemic and subsequent global lockdown. However, its ancestors and most foundational roots can be traced much further back, retroactively making many great classics some of the best cozy fantasy titles in history.

Whether we're talking about one of those hyper-influential classics like The Hobbit, or about a modern gem like Emily Wilde’s Encyclopaedia of Faeries, one thing remains certain: for people who love fantasy as a whole, cozy fantasy can be a delightful blast. It's all about comforting, relatively low-stakes narratives, as well as emotional slice-of-life moments favored over epic wars or world-ending threats.

Written by Ursula Vernon under the pseudonym T. Kingfisher, A Wizard's Guide to Defensive Baking was a major critical and commercial success upon release, winning awards as prominent as the Locus Award for Young Adult Fiction. Its story follows Mona, a teenage baker witch who discovers a dead body in her aunt's shop and must use her powers to foil a city-wide conspiracy and defend her home.

It's one of the best fantasy books of the 2020s so far, and with its being published right at the peak of pandemic lockdowns, it was one of the first books to be labeled as cozy fantasy by social media as the term was being born. Striking the perfect balance between warm domestic comfort, comedic charm, and real emotional stakes, it's an unforgettable quirky YA novel that even older fantasy readers love.

Heather Fawcett's popular debut novel, Emily Wilde's Encyclopaedia of Faeries, launched the Emily Wilde series, one of the best cozy fantasy novel series to date. Its story follows the titular Emily Wilde, a grumpy Cambridge professor who travels to a remote sub-Arctic village to research elusive faeries, only to have her fieldwork upended by a charming academic rival who turns out to be a secret fae prince.

The book makes flawless use of the epistolary format, making its whimsical folklore elements feel all the more genuine and enhancing the warmth of its cozy atmosphere. It's dark academia meets cottagecore, with a delightfully smart protagonist and a wintry aesthetic that makes the heat of every fire, the hug of every wool blanket, and the steam of every mug of hot cider feel as comforting as the real things.

The first installment in Olivia Atwater's Regency Faerie Tales trilogy, the Regency romantasy delight Half a Soul is one of the best fantasy books published since 2020. Its story follows Dora, a girl cursed by a faerie to be unable to feel or express emotions after losing half her soul. So, she travels through London with a royal magician to reverse her condition and solve a magical plague.

At first, the book was little more than a modest self-published release, but the cozy fantasy phenomenon on BookTok during the pandemic lockdowns gradually turned it into a huge success, leading Orbit Books to acquire and publish it in 2022. It's just that good: with its uniquely compelling heroine and her fun dynamic with the Royal Sorcerer Elias, Half a Soul is a celebration of empathy, kindness, and whimsy.

Perfect for people who aren't really looking to commit to a new fantasy series and would rather just enjoy a standalone cozy fantasy title, Sangu Mandanna's The Very Secret Society of Irregular Witches should be a joy to read. It follows Mika Moon, a lonely witch who breaks the strict rules of her society by posting online magic videos. The videos lead her to being hired to teach three unruly orphan witches at a remote estate, where she unexpectedly finds family and love.

It's the ultimate "found family" cozy fantasy book, a novel that's just about as close as you can get to a proverbial warm hug on a cold, rainy night. Full of low-stakes domestic magic and imbued with a bright, colorful atmosphere that makes the whole story feel safe and inviting, it's a book all about the deeply relatable theme of overcoming isolation and finding a place where it feels like you belong.

The first book in T.J. Klune's Cerulean Chronicles is The House in the Cerulean Sea, a YA story about a government caseworker who oversees magical youth. He's sent to evaluate a remote island orphanage housing six classified children — including the Antichrist — where he unexpectedly finds a family and a home. As great as its sequel is, it doesn't get much cozier than this.

House in the Cerulean Sea is a deeply emotional tale about acceptance and found family, full of romance, low stakes, emotional warmth, and a gentle narrative. All in all, it's one of the best fantasy books of the past 10 years, built on the foundations of a protagonist whose redemption arc is as satisfying as it is moving. The magical children are irresistibly sweet, the romantic elements work wonderfully, and the message against prejudice and institutional oppression is profoundly powerful.

If there's any one author who has done more than enough to be worthy of the title of "best fantasy writer currently working," it's the infinitely prolific Brandon Sanderson. The first of the four secret novels that Sanderson wrote during the COVID-19 pandemic, Tress of the Emerald Sea is one of the author's best books to date, following a young girl who stows away on pirate ships across the ocean to rescue her beloved from an evil sorceress.

Though an epic adventure is what fuels the narrative of Tress of the Emerald Sea, it's nonetheless the coziest piece of fantasy that Sanderson has thus far written. Whimsical, stylish, and deeply comforting in spite of its epic narrative, this first installment in the Hoid's Travails series (part of Sanderson's overarching Cosmere universe) wears its cozy Princess Bride influences on its sleeve.

As far as modern books that were specifically written under the newly-coined "cozy fantasy" umbrella go, it doesn't get much better or more iconic than Travis Baldree's Legends & Lattes. A finalist for the 2023 Nebula and Hugo Awards, this is the story of a battle-weary orc retiring from her adventures and moving to the city of Thune, where she accomplishes her wish of opening a coffee shop.

It's the ultimate cozy fantasy premise, and the way it's executed is every bit as warm as the silky lattes served at Viv's café. Proof that you don't need sky-high stakes in order to craft a high fantasy story that's thoroughly engaging, Legends & Lattes is bolstered by Baldree's beautifully tactile and comforting prose, the heartwarming "found family" story, and the welcoming subversions of traditional Hero's Journey heroic fantasy tropes.

British writer Sir Terry Pratchett was one of the most beloved satirists in the modern history of English-language literature, revolutionizing comedic fantasy with his massive Discworld series. Going Postal is the 33rd book in the series, and it follows a condemned con artist who's spared from hanging and forced to revive the city's ruined post office, while battling a ruthless communication monopoly.

It's one of the most fun fantasy books of all time, and it's all anyone should need to read in order to understand why Pratchett is such a beloved figure—though it'll inevitably become tempting to pick up some other Discworld installment. Going Postal may predate the birth of the cozy fantasy trend, but its masterclass in satirical humor, quirky industrial fantasy, and cozy approach to sociopolitical critique make it an essential ancestor for the subgenre's modern form.

Diana Wynne Jones' Howl's Moving Castle achieved modest acclaim and success among children's fantasy fans and critics back in 1986, though it would be the release of Hayao Miyazaki's 2004 anime film adaptation that would really push its reputation into the stratosphere. Its story follows Sophie, an 18-year-old who's turned into an old woman by a witch. She takes refuge in the castle of a vain wizard named Howl, where she bargains with a fire demon to break their curses and untangle a web of magical chaos.

It's one of those perfect fantasy books that turned into even better movies, and another one of the most important and noteworthy precursors of the cozy fantasy subgenre. Long before the term was coined, Jones established this blueprint for whimsical domestic magic, eccentric found families, and reluctant yet undeniably witty heroes. From the vibrant characters to the titular castle itself, everything about Howl's Moving Castle inspires a joyful sense of awe.

Even though it was The Lord of the Rings that would go on to enshrine J. R. R. Tolkien as the single most important and influential fantasy author of the 20th century, it was the smash hit that was The Hobbit that put him on the map as a master of the genre. It's the tale of the hobbit Bilbo Baggins, who's recruited by a wizard and thirteen dwarves to embark on a dangerous quest.

Even though The Hobbit was written nearly a century before the coining of the "cozy fantasy" label, and some purists may be scandalized by the mere proposition of considering it the grandfather of all cozy fantasy tales, it's nevertheless a subgenre that fits it too well to ignore. One of the most perfect fantasy books of all time, The Hobbit's story may have danger and adventure, but it's also the ultimate ancestor of the cozy fantasy category. Bilbo as a home-loving reluctant hero, Bag End as the coziest space in all of fantasy, and a story that celebrates the power of friendship and hospitality all make this the most foundational pillar of cozy fantasy.

Broncos proactively replace entire surface at Mile High Stadium, again

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

With four regular-season home games played and four to go, the Broncos are being proactive — again — about the quality of the playing surface at Mile High Stadium.

The entire field is being ripped up and replaced, despite the absence of any complaints about the quality of the grass there.

For the fifth straight year, the Broncos are spending the money to ensure that the players will be performing on the highest-quality surface, emphasizing that “the Walton Penner Family Ownership Group’s commitment to player safety and a best-in-class playing surface throughout the season.”

At a time when the debate regarding real grass vs. fake grass continues, with the situation boiling down to whether the owners are willing to write the check to ensure the best and safest surfaces for players, the statement made by the Broncos makes their ownership look good and, in comparison, owners who cling to artificial turf look bad.

Beyond caring about player safety, having the best playing surface provides the best protection of the investment the owners have made in their rosters. Which would be a sensible position for any owner — especially the cheap ones.

Now that it has morphed into a matter of collective bargaining, those who choose to be pennywise have even more cover for their pound foolishness. Which makes it even more important that the NFL Players Association apply not general public pressure to the league but specific scrutiny to the owners who won’t ditch artificial turf for grass, or who won’t replace low-quality grass with high-quality grass.

Vin Diesel’s 'Van Helsing' Replacement 'The Last Witch Hunter' Officially Lands on Prime Video

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Although he's best known for his work in the Fast & Furious saga, Vin Diesel has had a long and colorful career that leans into sci-fi, historical drama, superheroes, and action flicks alike. But on just one occasion, the international superstar ventured into fantasy territory with the criminally underrated The Last Witch Hunter, a film that recently left Netflix only to arrive on the free-to-stream platform Tubi. That isn't to say that The Last Witch Hunter is a phenomenal piece of cinema, mind you—but it is an action-based urban fantasy popcorn flick that absolutely delivers on the spectacle, and for that reason, it's worth a watch.

The Last Witch Hunter exists in the same vein as films like Van Helsing or Underworld. It's a fantasy that anchors itself in an "old world" but updates the material to the present day. It could also be compared to I, Frankenstein (which is maybe not the best selling point), but Diesel's action-packed low-budget fantasy is better on just about every front. The 2015 feature follows a lone protagonist, a widower named Kaulder, who has been cursed with immortality by the Witch Queen (Julie Engelbrecht), who he kills after she has killed his family. With time on his side, Kaulder chooses to use his new curse to spend his days hunting down the sorcerers who have made the world a living hell. To help in his quest, Kaulder has allied himself with an organization called the "Axe and Cross," which assigns him a new "Dolan" every lifetime, from Michael Caine at the beginning to newcomer Elijah Wood. Of course, that begins to change when Chloe (Rose Leslie) enters the picture.

For what it is, The Last Witch Hunter is actually a lot of fun. Not only is it a unique take on the witch-hunter concept, but the whole thing was grounded in Disel's own love for the fantasy genre. When speaking to HitFlix prior to the release of the film, the star revealed that his own experience growing up with Dungeons & Dragons was what inspired the titular character in the first place. Long before Dungeons & Dragons: Honor Among Thieves made waves in 2023, Diesel met with screenwriter Cory Goodman (who had previously written The Searchers-inspired vampire thriller Priest), and the pair bonded over their shared love of D&D. The result was a movie inspired by Diesel’s own character Melkor the Witch Hunter, "a name that obviously I stole from The Silmarillion," Diesel added — and who hasn't stolen from Tolkien for D&D? (This author once named his dragon hunter "Eru Ilúvatar," if that tells you anything.) With plenty of rich fantasy lore at its center, this fast-paced, visually stunning, and creature-heavy action flick is 106 minutes you won't mind spending with Vin Diesel.

The Last Witch Hunter may not have been well-received by critics or audiences, but the action is great and the special effects are nothing to write off, either. Even if Diesel's performance leaves a bit to be desired, it's hard to ignore the care and personal connection he put into bringing Kaulder's story to life. Perhaps what comes as the biggest surprise, however, is that The Last Witch Hunter is returning for more. Vin Diesel is working on a sequel, one that is set to bring even Michael Caine out of retirement to reprise his role as Dolan 36. Will it be able to live up to the wackiness of the original? Who knows, but considering many of us wish movies like Van Helsing had gotten their own sequels back in the day (or at least a television spin-off), those who enjoyed this supernatural thriller will certainly be happy.

So, if you're looking for some mindless entertainment to enjoy on an otherwise free evening, give The Last Witch Hunter a go. It's an oddball take on the genre, but one with a rich mythology and some impressive visuals that make it worth the watch. Although Halloween is over, it's still fall, meaning that now is just as good a time to return to this strange supernatural world than now — especially now that you can stream it for free.

The Last Witch Hunter is available for streaming on Tubi.

Conclave’ Director Edward Berger Teases Upcoming Brad Pitt A24 Film ‘The Riders’: It’s a ‘European Odyssey’

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

The renowned Swiss-Austrian filmmaker was this year's recipient of the Evolution Vision Award at the Evolution Mallorca International Film Festival

“All Quiet on the Western Front” director Edward Berger gave a brief update on “The Riders,” his upcoming A24 film starring Brad Pitt. Speaking at Evolution Mallorca, where he is the recipient of this year’s Evolution Vision Award, the filmmaker confirmed they have already shot the film, which he calls a “singular perspective story told through the eyes of Brad Pitt.”

“It’s a story of a man who’s renovating a cottage in the ’80s, in Ireland, waiting for his daughter and wife to show up,” he said of the story, which is adapted from the eponymous novel by Tim Winton. “Only the daughter shows up, and then they go on a European Odyssey to find [the wife.] [They go] to Greece, Brussels, Amsterdam… I won’t tell you what happens or if he finds her [laughs].” Related Stories 'Harry Potter' HBO Series Casts Sorting Hat, Nearly Headless Nick, and More Hogwarts Ghosts and Portraits HBO Max Psycho Thriller ‘Infamy,’ With 'She Walks in Darkness' Star Susana Abaitua, Set for South Festival Premiere

A24 financed and will distribute the upcoming film. Frequent Luca Guadagnino collaborator David Kajganich (“Bones and All,” “Suspiria”) penned the screenplay, and producers include Ridley Scott’s Scott Free, Berger’s Nine Hours, Pitt’s Plan B, and Kajganich. Further cast includes Julianne Nicholson, Danny Huston, Camille Cottin, Michael Smiley, and Lara Pulver.

The director also briefly mentioned “The Riders” when speaking about one of his earlier films, the Berlinale breakout “Jack.” Both films have seen Berger work with children, with “Jack” starring then 12-year-old Ivo Pietzcker and “The Riders” starring seven-year-old Coco Greenstone as Pitt’s on-screen daughter.

“You have too many needs,” he recalled of shooting with a child star for “Jack.” “[Pietzcker] was shooting for six or eight weeks, so he needs a tutor for school, he needs someone to prep him […] The hard thing is the clock is running. We had an exemption from the city where we could shoot with him for six hours, and usually it’s five hours. Every day I literally got the shot one minute before the six hours. It’s really stressful.”

“And we just made a film with another kid,” he added of “The Riders.” “It was the same thing. It’s like every day you just get it just before they take them.”

Elsewhere in the career-spanning conversation at the leading Mallorcan festival, Berger spoke about some of his most successful films, including the four-time-Oscar-winning war drama “All Quiet on the Western Front.” The director recalled being offered the project by a longtime producer friend and having an immediate negative reaction because he was at a point in his career where he “wanted to make an English-language film.”

“‘Conclave’ was in the works already and was percolating,” he added. “But in the back of my mind, or actually in the front of my mind, I thought: I can’t escape this. It’s something that I am going to have to do.”

The Austrian-Swiss helmer said he felt “we were at a time where, and it’s only gotten worse, where international institutions that had brought us peace for a long time were being questioned. Nationalism was on the rise.”

“You know, a hundred years ago, life seemed glorious too,” he continued. “We seemed to be at a similar point and it felt like something that international audiences could also be interested in because we don’t usually see these films from the country [that] had actually originated those wars,” he added of Germany, the country where he was born. “To bring the feeling of weight that I grew up with—or responsibility or shame—into that movie seemed to be interesting to me, and to share that with people from around the world.”

As for his follow-up, the also Oscar-winning “Conclave,” Berger said it took him “two, three months to wash off” “All Quiet.” “It was almost traumatic afterwards.”

“Every day I came home from the edit, went swimming in the lake and tried to wash it off,” he added. “It felt like so much stress, so much chaos, so much destruction, and I kind of wanted order.”

“‘Conclave’ was very much inspired by 1970s films, Alan J. Pakula, political conspiracy thrillers like ‘All the President’s Men,’ ‘Klute,’ ‘The Parallax View,’” he noted of his creative process. “Those three films were the example for it. They have great editing rhythm and framing [of] people within the architecture.”

Berger felt that precise rhythm was “fitting” for a story that started from the image of several older men sitting in a room for long stretches of time. “I was scared that people wouldn’t be interested in watching a bunch of people vote for one Catholic [laughs]. So how do I make that interesting, you know?”

The director said he would make new cuts of the film every week and show it to his friends during the edit. “And they’d tear it apart,” he said. “They just go like, ‘Yeah, this was boring, this was great. You gotta go faster through this.’ I don’t want to get that after the premiere. I try to get it as good as possible, and you just don’t know because you lose perspective.”

Sean Payton says J.K. Dobbins remains "a huge part of what we're doing"

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

It’s been a somewhat strange second year in Denver for veteran running back J.K. Dobbins. As coach Sean Payton tells it, however, everything is going as planned.

“He’s a huge part of what we’re doing,” Payton told reporters on Wednesday regarding whether the team is managing Dobbins’s workload. “So each week we look at — a lot of times who’s the back that’s in there with the hot hand. But he’s been fantastic. So to answer your question: No, that’s not a hot button topic with us here. It might be with you guys, but not with us. So we’re good. We’re good with the rotation.”

Payton is generally using a three-back rotation, with Dobbins joining R.J. Harvey and rookie Jonah Coleman. Still, with Harvey on injured reserve for Sunday’s showdown against the Chiefs, Dobbins got fewer chances than Coleman.

Coleman, who was on the field for 41 offensive plays, had 11 carries for 41 yards and four catches for 63. Dobbins played 13 snaps, getting seven carries for 13 yards and no targets in the passing game.

“Look, it’s always easy to work two [running backs] and it becomes more challenging when you’re trying to work three, but he’s doing a great job,” Payton said of Dobbins.

For the year, Dobbins has 94 carries for 306 yards in eight games, with eight catches for 27 yards. That’s 333 yards from scrimmage — an average of 41.6 per game.

In 10 games last year, before he suffered a season-ending foot injury during a Thursday night game against the Raiders, Dobbins was averaging nearly twice that amount, at 80.9 yards from scrimmage per game.

Through it all, the Broncos have been linked to a potential trade for Colts running back Jonathan Taylor. While the prospect of acquiring a workhorse would cut against Payton’s longstanding approach to use multiple running backs, Taylor’s arrival would surely result in even fewer chances for Dobbins.

Zillennials Don’t Feel the Need to Grow Up, <i><em>Club Kid </em></i>Knows Why

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Jordan Firstman’s deferred coming-of-age tale speaks to the realities of living in an unwieldy time

I’m 29, an age that has become a catalyst for conversations about what it means to be an adult, about settling down (or not), and, perhaps above all, how to show up for my own well-being (do I need to party less?) It’s a volley of topics that are often the subject of yearly think pieces, even when we know the conclusions they’ll reach. Millennials and Zillenials feel pressured by older generations to live a conventional and ambitious life, even as they’ve squeezed young people’s hope for a future.

The New York Times detailed this dilemma just last month, speaking to thirtysomethings across New York City who felt discouraged by their situation and, to put it plainly, left behind. The article pointed out that the long-running Harvard Youth Poll found, for the first time ever, that most young Americans don’t believe they will surpass their parents financially. In the last 30 years, the cost of living has skyrocketed, and, today, mounting loans and debt among young people have made a house feel out of reach. Looking even younger, Gen Z is feeling the brunt of the economy, with Fortune finding that two-thirds of parents say their Gen Z adult kids rely on them (and Playboy going so far as to connect the housing crisis to the sex decline). Why stop partying if the rent’s too high, if there’s no reasonable expectation that you’ll find security, buy a house, or live easily past 2050?

This growing frustration is likely why audiences—including myself—are connecting so deeply with influencer-turned-director Jordan Firstman’s sensational feature debut, Club Kid. The film offers a tender look at arrested development and unexpected fatherhood, but above all, it delivers an incisive look at the struggle to let go of youth when the world feels increasingly grim. The idea of partying instead of worrying is alluring: to exist in a space free from responsibility. Peter (Firstman), a thirty-something party promoter deeply enmeshed in New York’s underground queer club scene, lives in the apartment his late mother left him, sells drugs on the side, and his only real daily concern is recovering for the next party. The moment he crosses the threshold of any club, he plunges into a fugue state fueled by drugs and sex. Peter clings to his youth (his name aptly echoing Peter Pan syndrome), shirks all responsibilities, and actively defers a much-needed inward look.

Peter’s wake-up call arrives as a 10-year-old boy named Arlo (Reggie Absolom)—a son he unknowingly fathered years after hooking up with a man who insisted they turn their dalliance into a threesome with a woman. A decade after that fateful night, the woman’s best friend (played with drug-fueled frenzy by Kirby Howell-Baptiste) shows up at his door, a young child in tow, with news that the mother has died by suicide and Peter needs to step up. It’s a parable tailor-made for a worn-down generation that sees no reason to stop the party. It takes a ketamine stork of sorts to change course.

Club Kid revels in the growing pains. Firstman avoids reducing the conflict to a simple clash over parenting Arlo or his personal struggle to break free from the party lifestyle; instead, Club Kid unfolds as a tender parallel coming-of-age, where Peter grows up as he learns to parent Arlo, a quiet, curious kid grappling with the loss of his mother. Firstman embraces the missteps, letting us see the beauty of his life as it intermingles with his newfound parenting. The whirlwind of responsibility—of unwitting fatherhood—plays like a fairy tale, a wondrous reality that feels increasingly distant from what a thirtysomething can reasonably expect today.

Watching Club Kid, I considered my own parents, who conceived me (by happy accident, I’m told) at 27 and 29. They worked at a pizza restaurant together before opening their own bar. My childhood felt wrapped up in Atlanta’s nightlife scene; somehow, completely inexperienced, they still learned to balance their lifestyle with their newfound roles as parents. I lived as the only kid (with my two-years-younger brother) in my parents’ world for several years, often sitting with the bar regulars for hours while my parents worked, or being actively watched over by the cooks. It felt like tapping into another world, but also brought us closer. They didn’t give up their life for me; they made me a part of it. These days, this story sounds like a romantic ideal, a whirlwind adventure only possible without looming loans and the high cost of living. I simply couldn’t imagine doing the same today.

What makes Peter’s story compelling is how he navigates the responsibilities of fatherhood without fully forgetting himself. Some critics have challenged the film, claiming that it’s stigmatizing the queer party scene, but in his telling, he doesn’t rebuke his former lifestyle, just the parts of it that were bad for him. His friend even welcomes the news of his fatherhood with excitement, spending time with Arlo without passing judgment. Even if he eventually needs to distance himself from these friends’ party habits, it doesn’t need a blow-out fight. Life happens, and people move on.

That’s why it’s important that Firstman returns to the real problem at the end of the movie. A vindictive call to social services by Peter’s ex-best friend (Cara Delevigne) leads to Arlo’s deportation and the new father-son duo’s separation. It brings to light the real-life anxieties looming over this heartwarming story. Sure, Peter did not file the proper adoption papers, and his lack of money becomes damningly apparent when he stares down health-care premiums and legal bills in the face of an unrelenting bureaucracy.

Firstman refuses to provide a pretty picture; he instead studies the anxieties of millennials, a generation perennially unsure of the world around them, and distills them into a heartwarming, deferred coming-of-age story. Firstman dreams of who we can become, if only given the chance to grow up in a world where steady ground is hard to find. Peter is growing up alongside his son, hiccups and all.

Doris Salcedo Traces the Shape of Memory

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

The Colombian artist reflects on the state’s attempts to erase history, and the people’s continued refusal. Also: How should artists decide whether to participate in documenta 16?

In the face of decades of violence and erasure in Colombia, artist Doris Salcedo has made memory her medium. Senior Editor Valentina Di Liscia writes that her poignant sculptures evoke the victims of the devastating guerrilla conflicts “not by simply remembering or looking back, but by delicately articulating the contours of their absence.” Recently, the Colombian government drew ire for removing LGBTQ+, leftist, and feminist objects from the Museo Nacional in Bogotá, later reinstalled after public protests. Di Liscia spoke with Salcedo about the significance of that resistance, the generational wounds of political violence, and how Colombian artists are building paths forward, as they always have.

Meanwhile, artists around the world are grappling with the upcoming edition of the embattled documenta. Two anonymous artists who participated in the last iteration write candidly about the vicious attacks they endured from German media and politicians for opposing the genocide in Palestine. Their practical advice is for artists who are on the fence about showing their work at documenta 16, but it’s also an essential guide to navigating institutional networks that seek to quash meaningful political dissent, the very kernel of artmaking and creativity.

Today, we also report on an investigation into an Israeli airstrike in Lebanon that killed artist Ali Cherri’s parents in 2024, an overdue step toward justice, and the sentencing of Daniel Sikkema, who was found guilty of ordering the murder of his husband, gallerist Brent Sikkema.

More below, including glimmers of hope from the midterms — Claire Valdez will be your artist in Congress, after all.

—Lakshmi Rivera Amin, associate editor, books

On October 15, dozens of objects were removed from display at the Museo Nacional in Bogotá under the orders of the country’s far-right president, Abelardo de la Espriella. Among them were artworks and materials related to LGBTQ+ and feminist movements; the country’s 2016 peace agreement with the Revolutionary Armed Forces of Colombia (FARC); and the campaigns of left-wing leaders Carlos Pizarro and Bernardo Jaramillo Ossa, both assassinated in the 1990s, whom de la Espriella has called “terrorists.” The objects were reinstated within two weeks by a court order, but the incident roiled the nation: Hundreds took to the streets in protest and faced police repression.
