Timothée Chalamet Explains ‘Dune: Part Three’ Is “Its Own Thing” As He Praises Ending

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

As Denis Villeneuve prepares to release the conclusion of his Dune trilogy, Timothée Chalamet is praising the ending of the epic saga.

The 4x Oscar nominee recently teased that he’s “absolutely” satisfied with Villeneuve’s third installment in his big-screen adaptations of Frank Herbert’s science-fiction novels, which premieres Dec. 18 in theaters.

“It didn’t feel like the bar dropped at all, Denis just changed the location of the bar,” said Chalamet, who reprises his role as Paul Atreides, to The New York Times. “This movie is its own thing.”

Taking inspiration from Herbert’s Dune Messiah (1969), Chani star Zendaya noted of the sequel, “There’s a lot in this film that is very true to the source material, and there are departures.” Watch on Deadline

Robert Pattinson, who plays Scytale, added, “You got to be kind of crazy if you’re going to be picking fault with this one.

“The script was really cool, but it was quite complicated. And watching the film, it’s not about what I thought it was going to be about at all,” explained Pattinson. “It reads as much more of a grand political, moral fable, and the movie is so much more emotional. A lot of it is about parenting, and I just thought it was so moving.”

Set nearly two decades after Paul Atreides (Chalamet) seized control of the Imperium, Dune: Part Three sees the ruthless emperor confront the true cost of power as a sweeping conspiracy emerges, at which Chani (Zendaya) is at the center.

10 Magical Christmas Traditions Around the World

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Living far away from family is hard at the best of times, but it becomes particularly difficult around the holidays. If there's one thing that makes it a little easier for me, it's being able to mix and match festive traditions from different countries to create something that's uniquely ours. As an Italian living in France with a British husband, I love bringing together the best of each culture for a rather extravagant Christmas. On Christmas Eve, we have oysters and Champagne, as per French tradition, followed by an Italian feast and plenty of Christmas crackers—although I draw the line at Christmas pudding, preferring a simple pandoro over anything else.

Whether, like me, you spend Christmas far from your family, are planning to chase the sun this December or are driving home for the holidays, one of the great joys of Christmas is making memories and indulging in nostalgic traditions. All over the globe, different countries and cultures celebrate the season in different ways, marking the holiday with long-established rites and rituals or merging heritage to create beautiful new traditions. Below, we run through some of our favourite Christmas traditions around the world, and ask the Traveller editors about their own personal festive rituals. From seafood feasts on Christmas Day in Australia to surfing Santa’s taking to the waves in SoCal, these are the most wholesome traditions for some festive joy this season.

Christmas is not recognized as a national holiday in Japan, but the festive season is still celebrated—albeit slightly differently from how you’d expect. As part of a marketing campaign launched in 1974, a few years after KFC first opened in Japan, the brand came up with the slogan ‘Kentucky for Christmas’, and it stuck. On Christmas Day, many Japanese families tuck into a full feast of fried chicken—a tradition so popular that you often have to order weeks in advance to make sure you’ve got your bucket guaranteed for the big day. The original Christmas meal even came with a bottle of wine. Christmas cake is just as surprising in Japan, too—strawberry shortcake, made with layers of sponge, whipped cream and fresh strawberries, is a festive favourite.

As you might imagine, food is at the heart of Christmas celebrations in Italy. Every region has its own traditions and dishes, enjoyed on Christmas Eve, Christmas Day and throughout the festive season. While northern Italians might tuck into tortellini in broth and other comforting dishes, in the south we go all out. In my family, we have twelve different dishes—mostly fish-based—served for Christmas Eve dinner, before indulging in traditional desserts and an unholy amount of nuts while playing tombola. If there is one tradition that the whole country shares, it's the rather quirky tradition of La Befana – a witch-like old woman who flies through the skies on a broomstick on the night of January 5, leaving gifts for children to discover on the morning of 6 January, known as Epiphany (Epifania in Italian). Well-behaved children receive sweets and presents, while naughty ones get coal – although these days it's usually sweet, sugary charcoal, which is actually delicious.

The real difference between the Scottish festive season and that of much of the rest of the world is Hogmanay. Once Christmas Day is done and dusted, the focus is on New Year’s—or, as the Scots call it, Hogmanay. Street parties in cities such as Edinburgh roll on until the early hours, Robert Burns’ Auld Lang Syne is sung at midnight, and the ‘first-footing’ custom takes place—the first visitor to a home after midnight is believed to bring good luck for the year ahead (along with traditional gifts such as whisky, shortbread or a lump of coal, of course). Traditionally, a tall, dark-haired man is considered the luckiest first-footer.

While much of the Northern Hemisphere is wrapping up against the cold, Christmas in Australia falls in the middle of summer, meaning festive celebrations often revolve around the beach, sunshine and outdoor gatherings. "The traditional Christmas lunch in Australia is usually seafood, a roast ham served cold, lots of salads and then a pavlova for dessert," explains Lauren Burvill, CNT’s senior commerce writer and resident Aussie. "After lunch the classic traditions are to play cricket in the backyard, or if you're lucky, on the beach. It's not unusual to see Santa on the beaches as well, often riding on an inflatable rescue boat from the local surf club. On Boxing Day the country's favourite pastime is to watch the cricket or the Sydney to Hobart yacht race."

Forget snowy landscapes and cosy fireplaces—in Southern California, you might spot Santa Claus swapping his sleigh for a surfboard during the festive season. "I grew up in greater Los Angeles, and though December isn't typically beach weather, something uncanny almost always happens on Christmas," explains Megan Spurrell, Conde Nast Traveler US’s associate articles director. "The temperatures spike to the mid-70s Fahrenheit with shining sun and many families, including my own, end up at the beach. But we also go to look out for a very SoCal figure, known as Surfing Santa. We keep our eyes out at Manhattan and Hermosa Beach, especially near the piers, though I've heard of surfing santas in Malibu and Santa Monica, too. Most are just local dudes with a Santa suit who reliably hit the waves on Christmas morning to entertain their neighbours (and get a surf session in)."

While many Western traditions see Santa leaving lumps of coal in stockings for badly behaved children, in Austria it's the terrifying Krampus who comes to punish them. As the folklore goes, the Krampus is a fearsome horned figure with a long, snake-like tongue, who accompanies Saint Nicholas on 5 December, otherwise known as Krampusnacht, the eve of Saint Nicholas Day. While Saint Nicholas rewards well-behaved children with sweets and small gifts, Krampus is said to frighten or punish those who have been naughty. Today, the tradition is celebrated with Krampuslauf parades, during which people dressed in elaborate horned masks and furry costumes take to the streets, particularly in Alpine towns and villages. The folklore even inspired a Christmas comedy horror film in 2015, starring Adam Scott and Toni Collette.

In Peru, Christmas Eve, known as Nochebuena, is the main event, with families gathering for a festive dinner and celebrations that often continue well past midnight. "We alternate years of spending Christmas in Southern California with my mom and sisters, and going to Lima, Peru, with my husband's family" says Megan. "Though Peru has plenty of traditions tied to Christmas, perhaps my favourite is the sheer fact (shared in many Catholic and Latin American countries) that the whole affair begins at midnight on the 24th. It can be torture waiting until midnight proper to dig into the feast of turkey and arroz arabe that sits out waiting, but the entire city explodes in fireworks when the clock strikes 12. And then the party doesn't stop until the wee hours of the morning, sometimes at a family member's house or at the city's bars and clubs. It almost feels like a preview of New Year's Eve, just a few days earlier."

Dumplings are a major part of Polish cuisine day to day, and Christmas is no exception. The traditional Christmas Eve supper, known as Wigilia, begins when the first star appears in the sky, with families gathering around the Christmas table for a feast that traditionally includes 12 meatless dishes. "My favourite part of seasonal eating has never been the Great British Christmas Roast, but instead 'Polish Christmas', celebrated by my family on Christmas Eve, a haphazard tribute to the Polish side of my family tree," says Olivia Squire, Traveller's features editor. "When I was a child, my grandmother used to make pierogi (Polish dumplings filled with mashed potato and cottage cheese) from scratch, which in more recent years has been taken over by my mum and I and usually involves a lot of swearing and tears. It's all worth it when we sit down to eat them alongside a vegetarian version of bigos (hunter's stew—an insult to my meat-loving ancestors), breaded fish and holy wafers, soundtracked by Carol's at King's, my English dad's addition."

Forget spending a fortune on Christmas presents—in Germany, the tradition of Schrottwichteln puts a rather amusing twist on exchanging gifts, with friends and family swapping unwanted, bizarre, or downright useless objects instead.

"For gifts, we follow the German tradition of Schrottwichteln (literally 'junk Secret Santa'), where everyone brings something completely useless, unwanted or gloriously ridiculous, often something they were given once and never knew what to do with. The more pointless, tacky or absurd, the better," Karin Mueller, Traveller's visuals director, tells us. "Our children have grown up with this ritual, and what began as a cosy dinner has become the highlight of the year. They never miss it. Now that they’re young adults, they bring their partners along, and it’s turned into this wonderfully chaotic, joyful, very-London celebration that I’m pretty sure they’ll keep going long after we stop hosting."

Across England, Christmas is celebrated with familiar traditions, from exchanging gifts to tucking into mince pies and singing Christmas carols. But each county and neighbourhood has its own customs, too. In some seaside towns, brave locals take part in a Boxing Day dip—sprinting into the freezing sea to shake off the Christmas excesses, often while raising money for charity. There's also the age-old custom of burning a Yule log, traditionally associated with good fortune and protection from misfortune. In Cornwall, some communities gather in local pubs for a traditional Cornish ‘shout’, joining together to sing folk songs and sea shanties.

"In the hazy betwixmas period, the days of lounging on the sofa and gorging on Terry’s chocolate orange are punctuated by one magical evening at a local pub for a Cornish 'shout'", explains Lucy Bruton, CNT's social media manager. "A shout refers to the community coming together to sing sea shanties and folk songs in the pub, and the Lerryn (where my family live in Cornwall) shout is a particularly special one to me. The tiny pub feels packed to the eaves with people of all ages belting out some of my favourite Cornish songs – from "Trelawney" to "Lamorna" and, of course, “Cornwall My Home”. It’s dark, cosy and a real slice of the magic of Cornwall."

A version of this story originally appeared on Condé Nast Traveller UK.

As the most discerning, up-to-the-minute voice in all things travel, Condé Nast Traveler is the global citizen’s bible and muse, offering both inspiration and vital intel. We understand that time is the greatest luxury, which is why Condé Nast Traveler mines its network of experts and influencers so that you never waste a meal, a drink, or a hotel stay wherever you are in the world.

King Ghidorah's Origin, Powers, & Godzilla Movie History Explained

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

The release of Godzilla Minus Zero will further solidify King Ghidorah's status as the greatest villain in the history of the Godzilla franchise. Hitting theaters worldwide on November 5, the upcoming sequel marks the ninth time a version of the three-headed golden dragon has butted heads with Godzilla in live-action. Ghidorah already had a richer history than all other Godzilla villains, with Mechagodzilla being the only one that came remotely close.

Over the last several decades, Godzilla has accumulated an impressive rogues' gallery made up of a diverse lineup of monsters. He's saved humanity from a giant lobster, a prehistoric dragonfly, all sorts of aliens, and even beings created from his own DNA. And some, like King Ghidorah, had to be defeated more than once. Because of his popularity, the monster has been revisited on multiple occasions, across numerous continuities. As a result, there are several different versions of King Ghidorah in the overarching Godzilla franchise.

Most movies that have included him maintain a high degree of consistency in terms of how he's portrayed. However, the King Ghidorah character is incredibly nuanced, much like Godzilla himself. Some films have offered different explanations about his origins and what his powers are, thus handing a level of flexibility to Godzilla Minus Zero for its take on the King of the Monsters' archenemy.

Although far from the first monster to fight Godzilla, he served as an early opponent to the King of the Monsters, fighting him for the first time in Ghidorah, the Three-Headed Monster. Released in 1964 as the fifth entry into the franchise and a direct sequel to Godzilla vs. Mothra, the movie used King Ghidorah to faciliate Godzilla's first-of-many team-ups in his cinematic history.

In the movie, Mothra has to convince Godzilla and Rodan to stop fighting and join forces to drive away King Ghidorah, an incredibly powerful alien monster who wiped out the alien race living on Venus and has since set his sights on Earth. Following Ghidorah defeat at the hands of Godzilla and Rodan, the villain returned for a rematch with the same two monsters in 1965's Invasion of Astro Monster.

In Destroy All Monsters, Ghidorah had an even more difficult path ahead of him when he had to fight Godzilla, Rodan, Mothra, plus an assortment of other kaiju. His next role, though, which came in 1972's Godzilla vs. Gigan, evened the odds by having Godzilla and his ally, Anguirus, square off against Ghidorah and Gigan.

19 years passed before Ghidorah was utilized again. The Godzilla franchise was rebooted in 1984 with a darker, less kid-friendly feel. Several of his allies and villains were gradually reintroduced into the franchise, with Godzilla vs. King Ghidorah giving Godzilla and Ghidorah their first one-on-one fight in 1991. The movie also famously gave Ghidorah a new mechanized upgrade, courtesy of the human characters - Mecha-King Ghidorah.

Ghidorah's next two movie appearances were significant departures from the past. Ghidorah filled a heroic role in Godzilla, Mothra, and King Ghidorah: Giant Monsters All-Out Attack, a movie in which Godzilla was the true villain. In Godzilla: Final Wars, Toho introduced Keizer Ghidorah, who wasn't technically King Ghidorah himself, but a considerably more powerful member of the same species.

Legendary's Monsterverse tried its hand at Ghidorah by making the villain of the 2019 Hollywood production, Godzilla: King of the Monsters. Recreating the monster cast of Ghidorah, the Three-Headed Monster by featuring Godzilla, Rodan, Mothra, and Ghidorah, the film saw the latter attempt to terraform the planet. Naturally, it was up to Godzilla to stop him.

Nearly every movie appearance from Ghidorah - including his first and only Hollywood film - has defined Ghidorah roughly the same way. Ghidorah, the Three-Headed Monster established Ghidorah as an alien threat, an origin that every movie except Godzilla, Mothra, and King Ghidorah: Giant Monsters All-Out Attack has kept. Being a film where Ghidorah was characterized as one of three benevolent guardians of the Earth, it remains an outlier in regards to the kaiju's portrayal.

Typically, his alien origins are important to the plot, such as when aliens controlled him in Invasion of Astro Monster and Destroy All Monsters. Legendary's King of the Monsters, for its part, didn't discuss it at length but heavily implied that his nature as an extraterrestrial being was the catalyst behind the destructive path he carved.

One particular movie went much deeper with his origin story. In Godzilla vs. King Ghidorah's telling of it, he doesn't begin the story as "King Ghidorah." Rather than exist as a singular entity, Ghidorah was originally a multitude of harmless, golden dragon-like creatures called Dorats. Hailing from a distant planet, the Dorats were collectively transformed into King Ghiodrah when time travel was used to make them take the place of Godzilla at the moment that he was to be bombarded with nuclear radiation during World War 2.

Similar to the situation with his origins, depictions of King Ghidorah's powers are largely consistent as well. Each of Ghidorah's three heads are capable of firing blasts of yellow lightning known as "gravity beams." They're not as powerful as Godzilla's atomic breath, but through continuous use of his gravity beams from all three heads, Ghidorah can indeed use them to inflict serious pain on Godzilla.

Godzilla: Final Wars' central villain, Keizer Ghidorah, possesses a stronger variation of the gravity beam called Destroyed Thunder, which actually allowed him to manipulate gravity.

Just as it is with Godzilla and his atomic breath, the gravity beams are the signature weapon of King Ghidorah. But he has other ways of fighting Godzilla too, such as his ability to fly. He can scoop up kaiju with his talons and has enough physical strength to carry them into the air before dropping them from extreme heights.

As for how dangerous that makes him, that tends to vary. Even though Godzilla had help on his side in Ghidorah's first three movie appearances, it was never proven that he wouldn't have been able to beat him alone. And Godzilla vs. Gigan did demonstrate that Godzilla could win a fair fight against him. The same was true of the 1991 and 2001 versions of Ghidorah seen in Godzilla vs. King Ghidorah and Godzilla, Mothra, and King Ghidorah respectively.

Based on how his fights with Godzilla typically went, King Ghidorah is an incredibly powerful Godzilla villain, but not quite as strong as Destoroyah or SpaceGodzilla. But his relative, Keizer Ghidorah, has a strong case for being the most powerful Godzilla villain of all time. In any case, there's every reason to expect an intense matchup before watching Godzilla Minus Zero.

‘How Are You Supposed to Have Sex with a Wicker Dick? I Wanted to See’

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

When indie film distributor Black Bear released the first trailer for Wicker, it hit social media timelines with a bang. Based on the short story The Wicker Husband by Ursula Wills-Jones, the film follows a small village fisherwoman (Olivia Colman) who convinces a local basket weaver (Peter Dinklage) to build her a husband out of wicker (Alexander Skarsgård). Fans, naturally, went a bit feral over the scenes of Colman and a basket-ified Skarsgård consummating their marriage so rapturously they break the bed. Eventually, a common joke emerged: “Will this be available in 4DX?”

Sometimes, for the pure of heart, prayers are answered. On Oct. 28, Black Bear put Wicker in 4DX for a one-night-only event, allowing audiences to experience the low-fantasy romance complete with water, wind, snow, and all the thrusting their hearts desired. But while the idea initially stemmed from a joke, the demand was real. Tickets sold so fast that theaters extended showings until Nov. 5. And the romance fans who threw their cards down first tell Rolling Stone that what they thought might be an absurd night out turned into a raucous, communal experience — one they say highlights what audiences might want more of out of romance movies.

If you ask New York City resident Robert Belpasso, 32, he probably should’ve realized how serious people were about seeing the film in 4DX when he tried to buy tickets. Showings would populate and then sell out as he was trying to click purchase. “Initially, it was impossible to get tickets,” he says. “It was like fighting like a Ticketmaster war.”

Another New Yorker, Leen Dweik, and her friends didn’t even blink when it looked like the only available seats were at a theater in suburban New Rochelle — a 30- to 45-minute trip outside of the city. Luckily, seats opened up at a Regal in Times Square, but it really didn’t matter. They had no plans to miss it.

“I’ve never seen anything in 4DX before — I’m not necessarily interested in having things sprayed at me,” Dweik, 29, says. “But it did feel like if there was ever a film to try it for, I wanted to try it for this one. It was so clear that everybody who was there really wanted to be there. Some people even made merch.”  Editor’s picks The 250 Greatest Albums of the 21st Century So Far The 100 Best TV Episodes of All Time The 500 Greatest Albums of All Time 100 Best Movies of the 21st Century

Tia Burkett, 33, gathered a group of friends in Kansas to see Wicker in 4DX after watching the trailer online. But she was surprised by how earnest the story was — and how aggressively her seat moved during certain scenes. “There was one point where I was like, ‘Oh, my gosh, I’m about to fall.’ That first sex scene truly felt like we were on a roller coaster and none of us were expecting it. I’ve never laughed that hard in my life,” she says. “The whole ‘getting wicked down’ thing was just something I wanted to be a part of.”

There’s an obvious titilatting element to the film that’s drawing an audience in via social media — the film’s marketing has drilled down on the idea that this movie is about a woman fucking her wicker husband. The film delights in its arborial sound effects, twigs cracking and limbs growing alongside other wood-like appendages. Who wouldn’t be interested to learn more?

Karen Turner, 36, already says she’ll see anything Olivia Colman is in. But the technical elements of the story did convince her to buy a 4DX ticket to Regal’s L.A. Live theater. “It’s utterly ridiculous,” she says. “How are you supposed to have sex with a wicker dick? That doesn’t make sense anatomically. So I had questions going in. I wanted to see.”

She’s not the only one who felt compelled to investigate. “The funniest thing that I saw the entire time was everybody trying to figure out if there was going to be ‘full wick’ or not,” says Molly S., 32,  who saw the film with Belpasso. “It started hitting the rumor mills that the European version isn’t censored, but the U.S. version is. Everyone went in [to] do boots-on-the-ground journalism. That was just hysterical.”  Related Content Alexander Skarsgård Embraces His Magnetism in the Rolling Stones’ Video for ‘Mr. Charm’ Alexander Skarsgard’s Queer-Biker BDSM Rom-Com Is Definitely NSFW ‘This Is Spinal Brat’ — Charli XCX Unleashes ‘The Moment’ at Sundance Charli XCX’s ‘The Moment’ Is Fiction, But Also ‘The Realest Depiction of the Music Industry’ She’s Seen

“And there was full-frontal wick,” Belpasso confirms.

“Very tiny — from far away,” Molly clarifies. “Well,” Belpasso adds. “Quite large.”

Post Covid-19 pandemic, audiences have returned to cinemas across the country, including a dedicated surge in 4DX interest. Still, the format typically only offers action films, banking on fans wanting to experience the swerves and jumps from battles, high-impact car chases, or daring stunts. Wicker’s earnings at the box office so far aren’t huge — the film has currently grossed $13 million globally on an initial $10 million production budget — which makes it a moderate success for its independent status. But the cult status of the film online, which has only grown since its 4DX foray, indicates there might be an audience demand that theaters — and filmmakers — have underestimated.

Gracie D. has believed for years that traditional Hollywood romance releases have had a chemistry problem that could be solved with some creative shakeups. The 26-year-old considers herself “very invested” in romances like Wicker that center sentient objects. “I’ve been reading the genre for years. It’s not new. There are books about sentient doors and lamps. I read one about a polyamorous romance between a woman and a pair of Crocs,” she says. “The big appeal for Wicker is that it’s silly and it doesn’t take itself 100 percent seriously.”

With romantasy — books that combine romance and fantasy elements like magic — is a top-selling book genre, it’s not surprising that fandom translated to box office results for Wicker. But while the film’s 4DX demand is proof that sex still sells, the movie’s deeper strength lies in its use of Skarsgård and Colman’s sex life to slyly critique the town’s assumptions about female desire and agency — something many of the moviegoers who spoke to Rolling Stone said they appreciated above all else.  Trending Stories Trump's Bizarre Journey Through the Stages of Grief as Democrats Dominated the Midterms Elizabeth Holmes’ Partner Did an AMA About That Shower Scene, His Sniffing, and ‘You Can See Everything’ Dane Cook’s Brother Robbed Him Blind. Now He’s Rebuilding His Life — And Gunning for a Comeback Trump Is Already Crying That He’s Not to Blame for Republican Wipeout

“In romance films recently, Hollywood [is] throwing two hot people at the screen and expecting you to believe it when they take their clothes off,” Gracie says. “Wicker is presenting itself initially as something horny and weird, but what makes it so enjoyable and popular is that it also has these themes of women wanting to be respected in their partnerships.”

“I think that there’s something really profound in the explorations of romance between woman and other,” Dweik adds. “And from the get-go, Wicker was committing to the concept of being freaky and horny. People just want more freak shit.”

The Chicks Recall ‘the Incident’ and Compare Bush to Trump in ‘Taking the Long Way’ 20th Anniversary Shows: Concert Review

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Senior Music Writer and Chief Music Critic

Natalie Maines does not normally talk a great deal in concert. This may sound counterintuitive to say, since objectively there is no person in music history more famous for talking in concert than Natalie Maines. But she has always been more of a quality than quantity gal when it comes to that, so her legendary 2003 comment about being ashamed to share a home state with then-president George W. Bush was more of an anomaly than an indicator that she or her bandmates are going to be prone to lengthy speeches at any given concert by the Chicks. Related Stories Prime Video’s Superb Muhammad Ali Series 'The Greatest' Makes a Star Out of Newcomer Jaalen Best: TV Review Hulu's Mother-Son Mystery 'Phony' Mutes Connie Britton's Charisma Under Visual Effects: TV Review

Fortunately, the short current tour the group is wrapping up was custom-built to feature a steady stream of personal asides, which are welcome, if you happen to be a fan of the trio as personalities and not solely as musical talents. The main reason for that is that the outing is officially a celebration of the 20th anniversary of “Taking the Long Way,” the 2006 record that won five Grammys in total, including a rare Grammys sweep of the top three categories, album, record and song of the year. Not much was revealed in advance about the exact format of the tour. As it turned out, the Chicks (nee: Dixie Chicks) were not only down for spending the first two-thirds of the concert performing “Taking the Long Way” in its 14-song entirety, but for providing between-song commentary on most of the tracks, along with background on the preceding events that ultimately led to this left turn of an album — including “the incident,” as the Bush flap has been euphemistically known in band circles for 23 years.

This made for a rare treat, as Maines, Martie Maguire and Emily Strayer sat on stools on a riser for an hour and a half and went deep into “Storytellers” mode, engaging in a mixture of somewhat pre-scripted and fairly spontaneous dialogue before nearly all of the songs, also sometimes putting up documentary film clips on the big screen to further illustrate what was going down in the studio in the mid-2000s. (Some of the clips were from their “Shut Up and Sing” documentary, but many were unreleased.) The show fell into that rare sub-genre many of us love from a veteran performer: the docu-concert.

But the other reason for some added chatter during the tour was the timing, which was coincidental (seemingly?), leading up to — and, in Los Angeles, overlapping — the 2026 midterm elections. Their three-night stand at the Dolby Theatre in Hollywood began Sunday, two nights before the nation went to the polls, followed by a show on election night Tuesday, with a wrap-up of the stint and the tour scheduled for Wednesday. (Wednesday’s show will be a make-up for the one that was supposed to happen Monday, but got canceled in the hours beforehand due to an unspecified band illness.)

Naturally, the topical events of this week were bound to inspire a few thoughts comparing the current climate with the heated temperature that resulted in a lot of attempted Chick-broiling in 2003-4.

“It was 20 years ago, and a lot has changed since then,” Maines told the Dolby crowd near the start of Sunday’s show. “Now I would give George Bush a big kiss. Now we’re ashamed the President of the United States from the United States.” (Maines had made similar remarks earlier in the tour, and noted that these had been publicized enough that the White House issued an official statement “calling me a peanut head… or peanut brain, I can’t remember.” For the record, White House spokesperson Davis Ingle said on Oct. 15, “Natalie Maines is a despicable nobody who clearly suffers from a severe case of Trump Derangement Syndrome that has completely rotted her peanut-sized brain.”)

The crowd of 3,400, skewed disproportionately toward progressive cowgirls, went nuts. As they did later in the first act, when, before singing “I Hope,” Maines shared that they had recently visited the Obama museum, which gave them hope of again seeing a country that is not “run by a deranged nutjob.”

The only other overt reference to current events came during the second act — the greatest-hits set — when Maines sang “White Trash Wedding” and followed the time-honored opening line “You can’t afford no ring” with the contemporary addendum: “Because gas prices are too high! And electricity. And medical!” They expounded upon this a little more during Tuesday night’s show, when all three members fanned themselves with large “Fuck Trump” fans.

There’s one word to describe the trio’s nearly quarter-century history of not backing down: Nice.

Not every artist who does a full-album throwback show plays the original record in order; some rearrange the sequence to put a big single at the end. But the Chicks adhered to original running order, which meant that “Not Ready to Make Nice” provided a natural climax to the show only three songs in. And anyway, sometimes gratification that isn’t delayed is the best kind. So when Maines broke from the visage of chagrin and anger that characterizes most of “Not Ready…” to bust into a defiant smile after admitting “I kinda like it,” we’d gotten the most cathartic moment of the show out of the way less than 15 minutes into a two-and-a-half-hour gig. And we kinda liked it, because after all, act-2 favorites like “Sin Wagon” and “Goodbye Earl” don’t exactly count as anticlimactic.

Any serious fan of the group might have heard those latter barnburners in concert enough for one lifetime… not that retributive murder and taunting fornication ever get too old. But the main draw of this show for a more hardcore Chick-aholic was as it usually is for veteran acts’ full-album gigs: the promise of long-ignored deep tracks as inherently part of the concept. And “Taking the Long Way” certainly has its recently unplumbed depths. Four of the songs from the 2006 album have remained staples of the group’s sets: the quasi-title track “The Long Way Around,” the balladic “Easy Silence,” the rocking “Lubbock or Leave it” and, of course, “Not Ready.” That left 10 the group has not gone back to in a show for at least 10 years and, in several cases, 20.

Or never. “Baby Hold On” is apparently the one track the group never performed live before this tour (at least according to setlists.fm). Four had not been played since the arena tour of 2016: “Everybody Knows,” “Silent House,” “Favorite Year” and “I Like It.” Another, “Lullaby,” had not mollified an audience since 2014. And four hadn’t been performed since the year the album came out, two decades ago: “So Hard,” “I Hope,” “Bitter End” and “Voice Inside My Head.” In fact, the last two of those had apparently only ever been performed live once. All to say: the Chicks were taking the long way back around to a huge part of this setlist.

How’s it holding up, anyway? Probably everyone can think of Grammy album of the year winners that seemed like a good idea at the time, to somebody, but haven’t dated well. And it would be wrong to imagine that “Taking the Long Way” made it into the winners’ circle solely on musical merits, when there was clearly an appetite to give the Chicks a medal of valor for all that they’d put up with on top of a slew of Grammys. Even I, not having listened to the full album in quite a while, approached this concert playback with a touch of trepidation: What if it didn’t live up to memories and it felt like the Recording Academy had given out all that gold as a consolation prize for their crucifixion?

So there was good news Sunday — not quite as good as Tuesday night’s national news, but still pretty good — in just how solidly “Taking the Long Way” stands up as not just some sort of political milestone but a superior collection of music. (I’d still give the nod to “Home” as their best, but this remains a strong second.) The dance the trio did in collaboration with producer Rick Rubin and their songwriting collaborators was a slightly tricky one, with enough themes of personal iconoclasm running through several key songs that it almost came to feel like a concept album, even if they were only being allusive to the controversy that continued to follow them… with the exception of “Not Ready,” which was pretty darned specific, given how only a minority of us can relate to receiving death threats.

That anthem was preceded by a film reel of right-wing commentators having their way with the Chicks in the immediate wake of the Bush controversy, including Fox host Bill O’Reilly famously opining that “these are callow, foolish women who deserve to be slapped around.” Maines recounted their first writing session with Dan Wilson, who co-wrote six of the album’s songs, where “he said, ‘So you probably wanna write about the incident… You wanna write like an apology song?’ We said, ‘Fuck that.’ And he was like, ‘OK, fine. How about “Not Ready to Make Nice”?'” The result gave songwriting blind dates a good name. “Even though we went through all the stages of grief, we landed on anger for most of it,” said Strayer, the trio’s banjo player, “and we thought it was so specific to our story that it wouldn’t necessarily relate to everybody else, but little did we know or anticipate that it would become such an anthem for everybody else’s story as well.” Now, maybe even more than then, there’s something about their righteous Troll Derangement Syndrome that rings a bell.

The members were able to point out instances in other songs that referred in some way to the controversy. While each verse in “The Bitter End” was about a different kind of parting, and in some cases a sweeter one, they confirmed that the final verse did indeed describe their immediate estrangement in 2003 from the country music establishment they’d spent years kissing up to, be it label execs or radio programmers or fellow country stars. The last verse rang afresh with a mixture of resignation and the triumphalism that is inherent in any of their three-part harmonies: “You had a good time / Drinking all of our wine / After the show / We all rode the wave / Of that crazy parade / Oh, where’d you go / What happened to / The ones we knew / As long as I’m the shiniest star / Oh, there you are… / We’ll still be here when you come round again.” That’s a family reunion that never quite came true, as the group came to be more accepted in rock and Americana circles than in mainstream country… though the number of hats and boots in attendance at the Dolby made it clear there was a huge part of the former audience that never got lost back when sides were taken.

There were some more amusing memories during this first section, as “Lubbock or Leave It” led to a discussion of whose parents were most supportive at the time. Maines’ father, famed steel player Lloyd Maines, “a father figure to us musically,” was seen in a film clip describing how he’d tossed off an angry letter to the hometown Lubbock country station that banned the Chicks — whereas Strayer and Maguire, who are sisters, had “our mom was saying things like, ‘I’m gonna put her (Maines) over my lap and spank her’ — stuff like that. So we had a little different dynamic going on at home.”

Some other family fighting came up. “I bet that’s the only time you’ve ever heard sisters fight about in vitro,” said Maines, following a film clip in which Maguire and Strayer got into it about the difficulties of scheduling dates on their European tour around her IVF procedures. “I had to go see a different gynecologist in a different country every other day,” explained Maguire, while her sister had already had success using the procedure. The resulting song, being performed on this tour for the first time in two decades, was “So Hard,” surely one of the first songs on a bestselling album devoted to in vitro fertilization. (Ironically, Sara Bareilles just performed a contemporary one on the same subject at the Dolby just a couple of weeks ago.)

And speaking of songs that very much capture a moment in time, Strayer pointed out that “Baby Hold On” referred to the three children under the age of 3 1/2 she and then-husband Charlie Robison had at the time, and the need to find marital intimacy amid that, when they sang, “Let’s kick the babies out of bed / How ’bout you and me instead.” Strayer and Robison would be divorced within a couple of years, which may account for why the song was never played live until this year. “I think I thought it was about hope, but it was probably more about desperation at that point,” Strayer admitted to the Dolby audience. “We’ve all been there,” responded Maines.

The album also had its share of less overtly autobiographical but still deeply personal-sounding songs. “Favorite Year,” co-written with Sheryl Crow, was one of these, a spirited but still haunting recollection of a past love affair that left a lot of “what if” questions behind, even though the singer has a husband and child. If this one had a personal meaning for one or more of the members, we didn’t learn, because there were a few numbers from “Taking the Long Way” when they did just shut up and sing. But “when we were together, I was at my best” is both a warm thought and a chilling one. And the song provided yet another example of how, on this album, the trio sometimes sounded a lot closer to the Bangles than anything out of Nashville or even Texas.

Come the end of the album playback, Maines said they would be “taking a brief intermission, and come back and play this whole album backwards.” Instead, the second act brought nine songs that are a part of any of their contemporary sets — including the mashup of “Long Time Gone” and “Daddy Issues” they’ve been including ever since they premiered it in collaboration with Beyonce on the 2016 CMA Awards. (Is it really the 10th anniversary of that controversy?) The only original song performed from the last 20 years came from their only album released in the last 20 years, the title track of 2020’s “Gaslighter” … a divorce song, not a Trump song, though it being played during the week that America effectively voted to divorce the president gave it a little extra resonance.

There was more rock and less talk in this final greatest-hits segment. Of course fans would hope that another album might be in the hopper from the music world’s least studio-prolific superstar act shy of Lauryn Hill. Perhaps collecting memories and overseeing the documentary footage for the “Long Way” retrospective has reminded them they like the recording studio? Or perhaps they will continue to lean on repertoire. They’ve got one of the greatest catalogs in contemporary music, so it’s hard to complain if they find a way to milk it every few years. They took the short way around to becoming instantaneous pariahs 23 years ago, but when it came to going from that panic-button moment to being transformed into heroines for the ages, it’s been a hell of a long tail.

Setlist for the Chicks at the Dolby Theatre in Los Angeles, Nov. 1, 2026:

The Long Way AroundEasy SilenceNot Ready to Make NiceEverybody KnowsBitter EndLullabyLubbock or Leave ItSilent HouseFavorite YearVoice Inside My HeadI Like ItBaby Hold OnSo HardI Hope

The 10 Best Disney Dances on 'Dancing With the Stars,' Ranked

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Dancing with the Stars has been entertaining audiences on ABC for more than 20 years. It turns celebrities into dancers while becoming one of the network's top-rated programs. The television show eventually began hosting theme nights, one of them being Disney.

The show is currently airing its 35th season, and this year, contestants danced to songs inspired by Disney movies that were developed into Broadway musicals. While it's different from previous Disney nights, it does not take away the importance of other dances from previous seasons. With that in mind, here is a list of the best Disney dances on Dancing with the Stars.

Due to Season 31 only airing on Disney+, the show changed things, and created a Disney+ night. Charli D'Amelio and her partner Mark Ballas were selected to perform a jazz number to the theme song from The Simpsons. The duo put on their best Marge and Homer Simpson outfits, and performed a showstopping number that made the Simpsons look like the perfect couple.

There were times in the routine where Charli appeared to be nervous, which is normal for all contestants who make it halfway through the season. Those nerves began to disappear when she and Mark performed simple moves, and the rise in confidence gave her the edge she needed to show audiences that she is more than just a social media personality. Her confidence boost helped her earn a 36 out of 40 from the judges, and find herself winning the mirror ball trophy without ever landing in the bottom two.

James Maslow and his partner Peta Murgatroyd celebrated the show's first-ever Disney night while competing in Season 18 by dancing a contemporary piece to "Let It Go" from Frozen. The routine brought out a gentle side of James that Big Time Rush fans had never seen before, and his relaxing facial expressions brought a calmness to a dance that wasn't over the top.

Peta was just as calm, and it helped her and James have incredible chemistry throughout the entire routine. Their loving embrace at the end of their performance was the cherry on top of a fantastic contemporary piece. The judges also loved the beauty of the performance, and awarded James a perfect score once he finished dancing.

Heather Morris already had dance experience before competing with her partner, Maksim Chmerkovskiy, in Season 24. After Maksim got injured, she danced with Alan Bersten, and the two performed an enthusiastic jazz routine to "For the First Time in Forever" from Frozen. It was unfortunate to see her performance lack jazz moves, but the pure joy she had throughout the entire routine is too much to overlook.

The happiness she felt while dancing was contagious to a point where late judge Len Goodman gave spirit fingers in his seat. Even though her performance lacked the kind of jazz the judges wanted to see, Heather was still able to earn 34 out of 40 points just for bringing her passion to the dance floor. Sadly, Heather's love of dance was not enough to spare her from elimination, and she was booted off the show a week later in one of the most shocking eliminations in the show's history.

Juan Pablo Di Pace gave off the most arrogance imaginable during the show's 27th season when portraying Gaston in his Viennese waltz to the song "Gaston" from Beauty and the Beast. He and his partner, Cheryl Burke, reminded audiences through dance that Gaston would never be husband material. The choreography was brilliant, and the build up to Juan hanging from a chandelier was priceless.

The actor's success in filling Gaston's egotistical shoes brought the performance to life. His portrayal outshined the other pros, and the fierceness he brought to the Viennese waltz was refreshing to see on a fairy tale evening. The judges were just as impressed with Juan's performance, and awarded him a 29 out of 30.

Rumer Willis and her partner Val Chmerkovskiy crushed their Samba to "Poor Unfortunate Souls" from The Little Mermaid. The actress went from top contestant to graceful sea witch in the blink of an eye. Everything from the shimmying to the overdramatic arm movements made audiences get to know Ursula as a creature, and not as a witch planning to conquer Atlantica.

Rumer also never came close to breaking character, which is extremely hard to do when having fun in a dance routine. Ursula is the villain fans love to hate, but the star's dancing made viewers forget why Ursula is a manipulative monster. The judges also saw past the evil in Ursula, and gave the soon-to-be Season 20 champion a 39 out of 40.

Four songs, four completely different theories about how a life is supposed to go. Here is the one that has been playing under yours, what it says about you, and where you have heard it doing the same job on screen.

Your life sounds like the most stubbornly hopeful song ever written. It is about ordinary people on an ordinary night who have not given up on the thing they are quietly working towards, which is a description of most people and almost nobody admits it.

Your life sounds like a training montage, and you are completely fine with that. This is the song for people who need a bit of friction to function, and who get noticeably better the moment somebody suggests they cannot do it.

Your life sounds like the happiest song ever recorded, and the clever thing about it is that it is quietly a bit sad underneath. You are the person who makes an evening happen, and the room is measurably worse when you are not in it.

Your life sounds like a song about getting out. Springsteen wrote the definitive account of being young somewhere small and knowing, absolutely, that the real thing is happening somewhere else.

Harry Shum Jr. channeled his inner Dick Van Dyke for a Mary Poppins-inspired jazz routine to "Super­cali­fragilistic­expiali­docious." The Season 35 routine was full of quick feet and fun quirks, along with umbrella skills one could dream of doing in musicals. He also threw in a few knee spins, which were similar to moves he did in dance routines while portraying Cable in Step Up 2: The Streets and Step Up 3D.

Although it appeared as if his partner Jenna Johnson would outshine him, that prediction went out the window after Harry smiled on the dance floor. He was also clever enough to show different emotions through his facial expressions, and it helped the choreography become more eccentric. Everything he did was practically perfect in every way, and he managed to earn his second perfect score in a row from the judges.

Normani did not play around in Season 24 when dancing a paso doble to "I'll Make a Man Out of You" from Mulan. The singer proved to be a warrior ready for combat the moment she stepped on stage with her partner, Val. Normani hit her moves spot on, and did so while listening to the vocal styling of Donny Osmond, who portrayed the singing voice for Captain Li Shang in the 1998 film.

There were a few occasions in the routine where Normani's intense facial expressions almost disappeared. The warrior in her did not diminish, but her smile was almost too big to contain. In a way, the singer's struggle was adorable, and the judges praised her and Val's epic routine before awarding them 39 out of 40 points.

Jordan Fisher and his partner Lindsay Arnold danced a stunning Foxtrot to "You're Welcome" from Moana. The routine was one of the most precise and relaxed dances of the season, and it was nearly impossible to tell who the Dancing with the Stars pro was. The mellowness also made the dance look simple.

Jordan's smile was a bit smaller than Lindsay's at times. He still appeared to be dancing on cloud nine, and may have gotten a kick from dancing to a song he sang with Lin-Manuel Miranda for the Moana soundtrack. The judges were more than impressed with the routine, and awarded Jordan a perfect score just weeks before he won the Season 25 mirror ball trophy.

Riker Lynch always had a smile on his face when he entered the ballroom...that is until he portrayed Captain Jack Sparrow for his paso doble to "He's a Pirate." The singer and his partner, Allison Holker, stepped out of a Pirate's of the Caribbean movie and performed one of the fiercest routines of Season 20. Riker outdid some of the best Dancing with the Stars pros in the routine, and his ability to keep up with the timing was impeccable.

Riker's acting abilities alone were phenomenal enough to make this one of the best routines on Dancing with the Stars. Once he and Allison were able to halt their pirate personas, the judges gave the pair a 38 out of 40. Riker went on to become first-runner-up in Season 20, and his routine continues to live on in fans' minds.

Jenna Dewan and her partner Val made the quickstep look easy during their Season 35 performance to "Be Our Guest" from Beauty and the Beast. The duo were in sync throughout the entire routine, and Jenna's confidence reminded fans why the power of dance can make a difference in anyone's life. Even though Val is the professional dancer in their partnership, Jenna dominated that routine, and proved that if she weren't an actor, she would be a Broadway star.

Besides confidence and dancing abilities, Jenna unleashed her personality whenever she could. It was little things like sticking out her tongue, trying delicious gray stuff, or pushing Val away with her foot that allowed audiences to get to know the real Jenna. The judges felt as much joy as her fans, and awarded the actress her first perfect score of the season.

We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.

New TMNT Sequel Confirms Radical Redesign For Classic Series Villain

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

The Teenage Mutant Ninja Turtles’ latest series is in full swing, bringing Leonardo, Donatello, Michelangelo, and Raphael back in peak form. While only two installments have been released so far, it's already one of our favorite takes on the Turtles to date. Now, the series has gotten even better with the debut of a classic TMNT villain, who has received a totally radical new redesign.

September saw the launch of IDW Publishing’s Tales of the Teenage Mutant Ninja Turtles: Coastal Chaos #1, the first issue in writer Andrew Joustra and artist Louie Joyce’s new TMNT series. The title Tales of the Teenage Mutant Ninja Turtles may sound familiar to some fans, given the two-season animated series of the same name that premiered on Paramount+ in 2024.

Paramount+’s Tales of the Teenage Mutant Ninja Turtles was originally intended to bridge the gap between the first Mutant Mayhem (2023) movie and its highly anticipated 2027 sequel. However, the series was ultimately canceled amid Paramount’s broader restructuring following its merger with Skydance. Meanwhile, IDW Publishing brought this iteration of the Turtles to comics in October 2025 with Tales of the Teenage Mutant Ninja Turtles.

Now, this iteration of the Turtles has returned in Joustra and Joyce’s Coastal Chaos, and it’s nothing short of epic. While the series has plenty of strengths, it’s Joyce’s art that truly makes it stand out among TMNT’s ever-expanding bibliography. His art style is bright, cartoonish, and just downright fun, giving the four brothers some of their best redesigns in recent years.

With Coastal Chaos #2 out and available to read, we’ve just witnessed the legendary Armaggon, TMNT’s anthropomorphic shark villain, rendered in Joyce’s exceptional art style. And it’s totally Cowabunga awesome!

While Armaggon was teased in Teenage Mutant Ninja Turtles: Coastal Chaos #1, he didn’t make his full debut until issue #2, as we only caught brief glimpses of him in the first issue. Coastal Chaos #2 sees the Turtles confront Armaggon about his recent beach attacks, calling out the shark for attacking Mikey while he was surfing and targeting other beachgoers. It’s here that we get our first proper look at the villain’s redesign, and while the character is instantly recognizable, this is also one of Armaggon’s most distinctive looks to date.

Armaggon originally debuted in the Archie comics in the early 1990s as a shark mutant from the future. However, many fans will likely know him from the 2012 Teenage Mutant Ninja Turtles animated series, where he appears as a cybernetic alien shark bounty hunter.

Armaggon is typically depicted as a hulking, anthropomorphic shark. However, Joyce flips the character’s traditional design on its head by giving the villain a relatively scrawny appearance that toes the line of sickly. With twig-like arms, a slender build, and skin ravaged by scars, this is Armaggon as we’ve never seen him before. This interpretation is wholly unique while still retaining classic elements of the character, such as his cybernetic weaponry.

The sheer amount of detail Joyce puts into Armaggon’s appearance makes this one of the coolest interpretations of the villain to date. Armaggon’s role in the narrative is also exceptionally fun, as the story somewhat feels like a collision of JAWS and TMNT. We’re confident this is the direction Joustra is taking the story, given that the synopsis for Coastal Chaos #3 teases a very JAWS-esque situation:

The Turtle gang find themselves STRANDED AT SEA on a SINKING SHIP with a HUNGRY MUTATED SHARK hiding just out of sight, ready to make TURTLE SOUP, which he can do because he has BIG MUTATED ARMS. How are they going to get out of this one?! No, really — if you know, please tell them!

If you haven’t dived into Coastal Chaos yet, bump it up on your TBR list. This is a true Teenage Mutant Ninja Turtles story that delivers pure fun and good vibes, and Joyce’s art alone makes it more than worth the read.

Godzilla is gearing up for an epic final battle against one of his deadliest foes before unleashing his most ambitious crossover yet in 2027.

Tales of the Teenage Mutant Ninja Turtles: Coastal Chaos #2 is now available to read from IDW Publishing!

Kim Kardashian Reveals She Eats "Prop Food" on All's Fair Set

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Kim Kardashian admitted she often eats the prop food on the set of Hulu's All's Fair, costarring Sarah Paulson, Glenn Close, Niecy Nash-Betts, Teyana Taylor and Naomi Watts.

For Kim Kardashian, all's fair on the set of All's Fair.

That's why she has no problem eating prop food while filming scenes with costars Sarah Paulson, Niecy Nash-Betts, Naomi Watts, Glenn Close and Teyana Taylor for their Hulu legal drama.

"It's real food and I eat it the whole time," Kardashian said during the cast's Nov. 4 appearance on Good Morning America. "I'll be walking by after they cut and I'm like, 'Can I take a plate of that pie with me?'"

And her castmates have objections to her unusual snacking habit. As Nash-Betts quipped, "Do not let that tiny body fool you: She eats everything."

"I said, 'This is prop food! Why are you eating this?'" she continued with a laugh. "This is the greediest woman I've ever met in my life."

But there's a reason why Kardashian's costars have dismissed prop food.

"We don't want to eat it all day," Paulson explained, with Watts adding, "It's a thing called continuity. If you eat it in the scene, you have to keep doing it."

However, their testimonies haven't swayed Kardashian one bit.

"Because I'm new to this, they tried to tell me, 'Hey, continuity. Pace yourself. Eat a grape or half a grape because you're gonna have to do that the same time 50 times,'" the 46-year-old shared. "By the end, I am like, 'What did I get myself into?' But I love it."

In fact, the Keeping Up With the Kardashians alum has even recreated some of her own snacks with Rice Krispies off set. As she put it, "Last night in my room, I made Rice Krispie treats because I wanted all the girls to try them."

And while the jury's still out on Kardashian as a cook, her costars have been thoroughly impressed by her acting skills.

"Her ability to remember her lines, she's so good with that," Taylor raved to E! News last year. "She'll keep reading it over and over and over again. It's dope to see."

Added Close, "She's a very sweet woman, she does her homework, she has great people around her, which says a lot."

To find out what other stars are snacking on these days, read on...

"Fruit and raw nuts. Recently I've made an effort to keep lost of fresh products and nuts on hand for snacking in-between meals."

"I am snacking so much while I've been at home! I think it's because I'm home and near my fridge all day. I slice up an apple and it fills me up between meals. I love the satisfying crunch and so do my kids."

"My go-to snack is canned oysters. I know that might sound weird, but I am absolutely obsessed with canned oysters. They are such a good source of protein, magnesium and vitamin C. They are so tasty and always fill me up. I eat them straight out of the can, but sometimes if I want some extra carbs, I'll eat them with crackers and the crunch of the cracker with the soft oyster is so good!"

"Carrots and hummus is always a fave with a handful of raw almonds and/or cashews."

"I always have a cup of fruit near me, usually with grapes or berries. For drinks, I like homemade green juice. I mix in BODYARMOR Lyte, Coconut for some sweetness and flavor without having to worry about sugar."

"Handfuls of roasted, unsalted almonds, a slice of sourdough bread with cashew butter and sliced bananas, a smoothie (mine usually have whole milk Greek yogurt, ice, a handful of spinach, some pineapple, whatever milk I have (right now it is Forager Coconut Cashew Oat), a banana, half an avocado, two dates, a dash of cinnamon (and sometimes a scoop of nut butter)."

"Hot Cheetos and fruit with tajin and lemon, yummy!"

"Fruit! I can't think of a more perfect food."

"Cheese and crackers with lots of cheese."

Then you get your peanut butter, melt it, add some honey and stir all the deliciousness together! This creates almost like a caramel consistency. You don't use nearly as much peanut butter and you avoid getting it on your thumb every time you dip. Brilliant, I know!"

Netflix's 42-Episode Sci-Fi Series Deserves Credit For Turning An Unknown Cast Into Stars

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

When Stranger Things premiered in 2016, nobody expected it to completely take over the pop culture landscape the way it did, or turn its cast of then-unknowns into Hollywood A-listers. Created by the Duffer Brothers, the 1980s-set series about a group of misfit kids discovering supernatural threats and government conspiracies in their small town of Hawkins, Indiana, was a smash hit for Netflix from the start. Across nearly a decade, Stranger Things ran for a total of five seasons, broke streaming records, and became one of the biggest Netflix shows of all time.

Despite its enduring popularity throughout its run, Stranger Things became a victim of its own success. As viewership numbers swelled, so did the show's scope, lore, and, most detrimentally, its cast. Though some newer additions became breakout fan favorites, like the adorkable Robin Buckley (Maya Hawke) or the rebellious Eddie Munson (Joseph Quinn), by the time Stranger Things ended, the cast was so bloated that several OG characters got completely sidelined in the series finale.

This is a shame because these Stranger Things characters are why people fell in love with the series in the first place, whether it was the nerdy D&D group or the telekinetic Eleven (Millie Bobby Brown). When it first aired, Winona Ryder was the only famous name in the cast, but thanks to these incredible performances, the Stranger Things ensemble is now a veritable who's who of celebrity talent.

Although everyone in the Stranger Things cast was already a working actor when the show premiered, most of the young stars were just starting their careers. Nobody knew who Brown, Finn Wolfhard, Noah Schnapp, Gaten Matarazzo, or Caleb McLaughlin were yet. Even David Harbour, who had been working steadily for over two decades before Stranger Things, was unknown to the average viewer.

That all changed quickly. After the lightning-in-a-bottle magic the cast created in Stranger Things season 1, Hollywood quickly came knocking, and most cast members soon found themselves involved in major film or IP franchises during theshow's run. Brown scored the titular role in Netflix's Enola Holmes movies and appeared in the Godzilla franchise; Wolfhard had starring roles in the blockbuster franchises It and Ghostbusters; Harbour joined the MCU as the Red Guardian.

Even Stranger Things stars who didn't join a franchise racked up some impressive credits as their fame rose, with Joe Keery appearing in the high-grossing action-comedy Free Guy, Matarazzo starring in Broadway musicals Dear Evan Hansen and Sweeney Todd: The Demon Barber of Fleet Street, and Sadie Sink (who joined in Stranger Things season 2 but is considered a core part of the cast) generating serious Oscar nomination buzz for her dramatic turn in the Darren Aronofsky weepie The Whale. To say Stranger Things ​​​​​​opened doors for the cast would be an understatement.

The Stranger Things stars haven't slowed down since the show aired its series finale on December 31, 2025. Brown and Ryder have continued to use Netflix as a home base, with the former starring in Enola Holmes 3 earlier this year and the latter set to appear in the cast of the highly anticipated Wednesday season 3. Sink made her MCU debut as Jean Grey in Spider-Man: Brand New Day and is set to reprise the role in Avengers: Secret Wars (2027) and 2028's untitled X-Men film, while Harbour won an Emmy for his performance in HBO's DTF St. Louis.

Other cast members are enjoying post-Stranger Things careers onstage. Just this month, Matarazzo made his debut on London's West End playing Mark Cohen in Rent, while Keery made his Broadway debut in Other Desert Cities opposite Julia Louis-Dreyfus and Ed Harris. While not all the Stranger Things cast members got to end the show that made them famous on a high note, the Netflix series is clearly just the beginning.

The 10 Best Whodunit Movies in Film History

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

There are very few genres that reward an attentive audience quite like a traditional whodunit. From the moment a body is discovered, or a will is read, or a dinner party takes a sinister turn, the contract between filmmaker and viewer is clear: someone here is lying, everyone has a motive, and the truth is usually hiding in plain sight. Deeply rooted in the golden age of detective fiction, these movies have given the genre a life of its own, and the best ones, such as The Thin Man, Murder on the Orient Express, and Clue, offer audiencesmore than a cryptic puzzle.

The greatest whodunits are exercises in precision, balancing a cast of colorful suspects, a tight-knit setting, and a reveal that feels both shocking and inevitable. Some are faithful to the traditions of a drawing-room mystery while others bend and break the rules entirely, but the one thing that unites them all is the commitment to the thrill of answering one question: who did it? From shadowy black-and-white classics like Otto Preminger's iconic noir Laura to sleek modern reinventions such as Knives Out starring Daniel Craig, these are the ten best whodunit movies in film history!

René Clair's adaptation of the world's best-selling mystery novel, And Then There Were None, written by the Queen of Crime Agatha Christie, undoubtedly earns its place among the greatest whodunits by doing what the genre does best: trapping its characters, and its audience, in a puzzle with no way out. The film follows eight strangers who receive an invitation from an anonymous host to a home located on a remote island, where they are picked off one by one, following the grim pattern of a nursery rhyme.

And Then There Were None endures because of its flawless sense of escalating dread. Clair balances genuine suspense with sly, darkly comic touches and lets the tone shift between menace and wit without ever losing control. The ensemble cast, including Barry Fitzgerald, Walter Huston, and Louis Hayward, gives each suspect a distinct personality, making every accusation feel plausible, and every death lands with weight. The movie also pioneered the "closed-circle" setting structure, which countless other mysteries would later borrow, ultimately proving why And Then There Were None is an influential cornerstone of the traditional whodunit.

Robert Altman's Gosford Park is an Oscar-winning love letter to Jean Renoir's classic French film, The Rules of the Game, and features an outstanding ensemble cast of notable stars as well as a witty, cleverly constructed whodunit with real dramatic bite. The movie centers around several British aristocrats and their servants who travel to a countryside estate for the weekend, where their plans of relaxation and fun turn into a hunt for a killer after their gracious host is found murdered.

Altman’s roaming camera and layered soundscape make you feel like an eavesdropper, catching fragments of gossip from both social classes that matter more than they first appear, making Gosford Park one of the best upstairs-downstairs movies. The ensemble cast, including Maggie Smith, Helen Mirren, Michael Gambon, and Clive Owen, delivers uniformly superb performances, while Stephen Fry’s bumbling Inspector Thompson gently parodies the genre’s detective tradition. Part Agatha Christie homage, part social satire, Gosford Park respects the whodunit’s pleasures while expanding what the form can say, making it an intriguing puzzle with real emotional and intellectual weight.

John Guillermin’s Oscar-winning classic Death on the Nile is a glittering, sun-soaked whodunit that proves Agatha Christie’s formula thrives with the right cast and setting. Aboard a luxurious steamer cruising Egypt, the famous Belgian detective, Hercule Poirot (Peter Ustinov), must solve the shocking murder of an heiress, Linnet Doyle (Lois Chiles), who is shot dead during her honeymoon, and nearly everyone aboard had reason to want her gone, most notably the jilted Jacqueline de Bellefort (Mia Farrow), who has been stalking the newlyweds.

Ustinov brings warmth and wry humor to Christie's famous sleuth, while Anthony Shaffer’s screenplay preserves the novel’s ingenious plotting. The solution depends on precise timing, misdirection, and a deception that few viewers spot, yet it feels fair on a second viewing. The suspects are a showcase of acting royalty, including David Niven, Bette Davis, George Kennedy, and Angela Lansbury, who each deliver showstopping performances. Between its collection of familiar faces and its alluring murder mystery shrouded in scandal, Death on the Nile is a murderously clever and gorgeously mounted whodunit that remains a benchmark for the all-star mystery, and is a must-see for any fan of the genre.

The Last of Sheila is a perfect whodunit written by Anthony Perkins and Stephen Sondheim​​​​​, who famously staged elaborate scavenger hunts and murder games for their Hollywood friends, turning their real-life pastime into a razor-sharp mystery. A year after his wife was killed in a hit-and-run, a Hollywood producer, Clinton Green (James Coburn), invites six friends to spend the week doing an elaborate scavenger hunt while aboard his yacht. As each guest receives a card bearing a secret, one by one the secrets are uncovered, and the clues begin to point toward real guilt.

What truly sets The Last of Sheila apart from other classic whodunits is its intricate plotting and the script’s insider wit about Hollywood phoniness, which adds bite. Herbert Ross directs with glossy, sun-drenched menace, and the cast is a delicious collection of acid-tongued egos including Raquel Welch, James Mason, Dyan Cannon, and Ian McShane, who all engage in a bitchy, quotable banter that makes the film as funny as it is twisty. Decades before Clue and Knives Out, The Last of Sheila stood as the prime example of how a whodunit could be both a brilliant game and a savage comedy, deeming it to be a significant contribution to the genre.

Rian Johnson's hit Knives Out undeniably revitalized the whodunit for a new generation of fans and did so by honoring the rules of the genre while gleefully bending them. Daniel Craig stars as a master detective, Benoit Blanc, who investigates the recent death of a wealthy crime novelist, Harlan Thrombey (Christopher Plummer), who is found dead after celebrating his 85th birthday. As Blanc sits down with Thrombey's extensive family of eccentric relatives, each of them proves to be a potential suspect with something to hide or something to gain.

Rather than withholding the truth until the final scene, Johnson reveals the central secret early and then builds suspense from a different question: will the guilty party be caught? That inversion keeps the story fresh while still delivering the satisfying twists that fans expect, and nearly every clue pays off on rewatch. The entire cast is irresistible, notably Ana de Armas, who anchors the film with real heart as the kind-hearted nurse Marta, while Jamie Lee Curtis, Chris Evans, Toni Collette, and Michael Shannon make the Thrombey clan deliciously awful. Funny, stylish, and genuinely clever, Knives Out stands proudly beside Christie’s classics while feeling entirely modern and original.

Instead of borrowing from the pages of great writers, director and screenwriter Jonathan Lynn took the popular board game, Clue, and, with no established story, turned it into one of the most gleefully inventive whodunit mysteries ever made. Set during a stormy night in 1954, six blackmailed guests arrive at a New England mansion for a dinner party hosted by the mysterious Mr. Boddy. When their questionable host turns up dead, the butler, Wadsworth (Tim Curry), leads them on a frantic search through the house as the body count keeps climbing.

The film’s boldest stroke is its trio of endings, which parody the genre’s tradition of the grand explanation while delivering a satisfying solution. Each version recasts earlier scenes, and the screenplay’s tight plotting makes the whole madcap structure feel fair. The comedic ensemble is a dream as Curry’s wired, fast-talking Wadsworth establishes the chaos, while Madeline Kahn, Eileen Brennan, Martin Mull, Michael McKean, Christopher Lloyd, and Lesley Ann Warren all land unforgettable moments. Despite being an initial flop at the box office, Clue has since become a beloved cult classic celebrated for treating the whodunit as both a genuine puzzle and a wild, farcical romp.

Otto Preminger’s noir classic Laura is a haunting whodunit that begins as a traditional murder mystery and gradually turns into a fascinating character study of obsession, control, and deception. Dana Andrews stars as a homicide detective, Mark McPherson, investigating the recent murder of an advertising executive, Laura Hunt (Gene Tierney), who was brutally gunned down in her home. As McPherson talks to Hunt's friends and associates, he finds himself falling hopelessly in love with the beautiful victim.

Laura is widely known for its mid-way plot twist, which upends everything and reframes the second half of the mystery with an unexpected cleverness, ultimately setting it apart from other classic whodunits. The suspects in Laura are a wonderfully poisonous lot, notably Clifton Webb, whose performance as the caddy but elegant columnist and Laura’s mentor, Waldo Lydecker, earned him an Academy Award nomination for Best Supporting Actor. Joseph LaShelle's Oscar-winning cinematography immerses audiences in an almost beautiful nightmare, while David Raksin sets the tone of a dreamlike atmosphere with his sweeping score, which has since become one of the most recognizable film melodies ever written.

W. S. Van Dyke’s pre-Code comedy mystery The Thin Man is the film that ultimately proved a whodunit could be as much about the detectives as the crime itself, making it a landmark entry in the film genre. Adapted from Dashiell Hammett’s novel, the movie follows William Powell as a retired private eye, Nick Charles, who is dragged back into sleuthing when an eccentric inventor, Clyde Wynant (Edward Ellis), vanishes, and his former secretary is found murdered. While Charles would rather be drinking, his wife, Nora (Myrna Loy), is thrilled by the prospect of a real case.

The mystery itself in The Thin Man is deliciously tangled, but Nick and Nora Charles are the film's secret weapon. Powell and Loy, who starred in thirteen movies together, have effortless chemistry, trading quips over cocktails with a playful affection that was revolutionary for its time. Their banter not only makes the film endlessly rewatchable, but it also set the template for the witty, happily married sleuthing couple who have been imitated ever since. Albert Hackett and Frances Goodrich's screenplay keeps the clues moving at a breezy pace, while the finale, a dinner party where Nick gathers everyone to expose the killer, is a classic quality of the quintessential whodunit. Smart, sophisticated, and consistently funny, The Thin Man spawned a slew of hit sequels and, even decades later, remains an undisputed landmark of the genre.

Billy Wilder’s Oscar-nominated classic Witness for the Prosecution is a courtroom whodunit adapted from Agatha Christie’s play of the same name that builds up to one of the most celebrated twist endings in film history. The movie follows ailing barrister Sir Wilfrid Robarts (Charles Laughton), who takes on the defense of Leonard Vole (Tyrone Power), accused of murdering a wealthy widow who had named him in her will. The case looks grim until Vole’s German wife, Christine (Marlene Dietrich), is called to testify against him, turning a seemingly open-and-shut case into a labyrinth of a mystery.

The film’s brilliance lies in how it turns the courtroom into a stage for misdirection. Every cross-examination, every shift in testimony, feels like a clue, and Wilder’s script, co-written with Harry Kurnitz, keeps the audience second-guessing who is lying and why. The final reveal is so audacious that the film’s closing credits famously ask viewers not to spoil it. Laughton is magnificent as the cantankerous Sir Wilfrid, whose sparring with his nurse between scenes provides delicious comic relief. Power is slippery and charming as Vole, while Dietrich is mesmerizing, delivering a performance of cool, calculated ambiguity that steals the entire show.

Sidney Lumet's adaptation of Agatha Christie's famous novel, Murder on the Orient Express, is the gold standard of the classic whodunit, and ends with a twisted reveal that remains shocking and morally provocative decades later. Stranded by a snowdrift in 1935, the luxurious train becomes a sealed room where the notorious Belgian detective, Hercule Poirot, played by Albert Finney, must unravel the stabbing of an unpopular businessman, Edward Rachett (Richard Widmark), whose past hides a terrible secret.

The film’s greatest asset is its dazzling ensemble. Ingrid Bergman won an Oscar for her small but unforgettable turn, while Lauren Bacall, Sean Connery, Vanessa Redgrave, and Anthony Perkins fill every compartment with star power. Finney’s fastidious, theatrical Poirot, buried under heavy makeup and a fussy accent, is a delight and earned him an Oscar nomination, making him the only actor to receive a nomination as Christie's infamous detective. Entertaining, handsomely crafted, and endlessly rewatchable, Murder on the Orient Express is a landmark murder mystery that set the template for every star-studded whodunit that followed, essentially solidifying its place at the top of the list.

Angry Birds 3 Trailer Reveals A Sonic The Hedgehog Cameo

Although it isn't a full-blown standalone release like fans have been hoping for, a recent trailer has confirmed that Sonic will, in fact, be returning to the big screen ahead of the fourth movie in the live-action franchise, which has left fans utterly shocked by the news.

While it would certainly be a funny outcome if Sonic did wind up playing a major role in the story, either appearance makes more sense given the fact that Angry Birds had an official collaboration with Sonic Racing Crosswords, adding Red as a playable character.

Cam Skattebo misses practice due to a hamstring injury

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Giants running back Cam Skattebo said he felt something in his hamstring near the end of the team’s Week 7 win over the Texans and it’s still an issue coming out of the team’s bye week.

Skattebo was not spotted taking part in the team’s bonus practice earlier this week and he was officially listed as out of practice on Wednesday. Head coach John Harbaugh said “we’ll see” when he was asked if Skattebo’s availability for Sunday’s game against the Eagles could be impacted by the injury.

Linebacker Abdul Carter (illness) also missed practice, but Harbaugh indicated there was no concern about his status for the game.

Right tackle Jermaine Eluemunor (calf), linebacker Malik Harrison (groin), cornerback Colton Hood (illness), wide receiver Charlie Jones (hand), and left tackle Andrew Thomas (groin) were limited participants. Tight end Theo Johnson (neck), offensive tackle Marcus Mbow (knee), and defensive lineman DJ Reader (knee) were all full participants for the Giants.
